\displaystyle=\frac{5+10t+2t^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\delta_{X,2}^{\prime}(t)+\frac{1}{3}\delta_{X,2}^{\prime}(t)}{\delta_{X,2}(t)}

(335)

\displaystyle=\frac{2t}{5+15t+12t^{2}+2t^{3}}+\frac{1}{3}\frac{d}{dt}\log\delta_{X,2}(t)

(336)

and

\frac{1}{2\pi e+t}=\frac{d}{dt}\log(2\pi e+t).

(337)

The integrand
\mathrm{pmmse}_{2}(X,t)-1/(2\pi e+t)
now becomes

\frac{2t}{5+15t+12t^{2}+2t^{3}}+\frac{d}{dt}\log\frac{\delta_{X,2}(t)^{1/3}}{2\pi e+t}.

(338)

The advantage in having the integrand in this form is that the first term is well-behaved (it decays as
1/t^{2}
), and the second term’s integral can be given in closed form

\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\log\frac{\delta_{X,2}(t)^{1/3}}{2\pi e+t}\right)^{\prime}\,dt=\log\left(2\pi e\left(\frac{2}{5}\right)^{1/3}\right).

(339)

Therefore, equation (
334
) becomes

h_{2}(X)=\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{2\pi e}{(5/2)^{1/3}}+\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{t}{5+15t+12t^{2}+2t^{3}}\,dt.

(340)

We use equation (
340
) instead of (
334
) for numerical computation. Note that this resolves the same numerical instability issue when estimating from data: if
\mathcal{S}=\{X_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
is a multiset of i.i.d. samples distributed according to
P_{X},
and if
U\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{S}),
we compute the estimate
\widehat{h}_{2}(\mathcal{S})=h_{2}(U)
of
h_{2}(X)
via an expression analogous to that in (
340
) where
X
is replaced with
U.

The procedure of obtaining expression (
340
) from (
334
) can be carried out for a general
X
and
n
such that
\mathbb{E}[X^{2n}]<\infty
and
|\mathrm{supp}(X)|>n,
as follows. Let
\theta_{X,n}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
be the polynomial that is the numerator of
\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(X,t),
i.e.,
\theta_{X,n}(t):=\delta_{X,n}(t)\cdot\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(X,t).
Thus, we have that

\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(X,t)=\frac{\theta_{X,n}(t)}{\delta_{X,n}(t)}.

(341)

We define the function
\rho_{X,n}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
by

\rho_{X,n}(t):=\frac{\theta_{X,n}(t)-d_{n}^{-1}\delta_{X,n}^{\prime}(t)}{2\delta_{X,n}(t)},

(342)

where
d_{n}=\binom{n+1}{2}.
By the analysis of the coefficients in
\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(X,t)
proved in Theorem
18
, we have that
\rho_{X,n}(0)=0
and

\rho_{X,n}(t)=O\left(t^{-2}\right)

(343)

as
t\to\infty.
In particular,
\rho_{X,n}
is integrable over
[0,\infty).
The following formula for differential entropy directly follows from the definition of
h_{n}
in (
254
).

Lemma 46
.

For any RV
X
satisfying
\mathbb{E}[X^{2n}]<\infty
and
|\mathrm{supp}(X)|>n,
we have the formula

h_{n}(X)=\frac{1}{2}\log\left(2\pi e\left(\frac{\det\bm{M}_{X,n}}{\det\bm{M}_{N,n}}\right)^{1/d_{n}}\right)+\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho_{X,n}(t)\,dt,

(344)

where
d_{n}=\binom{n+1}{2},

N\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1),
and
\rho_{X,n}
is as defined in (
342
).

A similar conclusion holds for mutual information in view of equation (
272
) that expresses
I_{n}
in terms of
h_{n}.

Lemma 47
.

Fix a discrete RV
X
with finite support, and a
2n
-times integrable continuous RV
Y.
We have that

\displaystyle I_{n}(X;Y)=\frac{1}{n(n+1)}\log\frac{\det\bm{M}_{Y,n}}{\prod_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)}\left(\det\bm{M}_{Y^{(x)},n}\right)^{P_{X}(x)}}+\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho_{Y,n}(t)-\mathbb{E}_{X}\left[\rho_{Y^{(X)},n}(t)\right]\,dt,

(345)

where for each
x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)
we denote by
Y^{(x)}
the RV
Y
conditioned on
\{X=x\}.

Note that in Lemmas
46
and
47
, the determinants
\det\bm{M}_{A,n}
and the rational functions
\rho_{n}(A;t),
for
A\in\{X,Y\}
or
A\in\{Y^{(x)}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)\},
are completely determined by the first
2n
moments of
A.
To obtain the estimates
\widehat{h}_{n}
and
\widehat{I}_{n}
given samples, the moments of
A
are replaced with their respective sample moments in formulas (
344
) and (
345
).

11.2
Consistency

As sample moments converge almost surely to the moments, and as our expressions for differential entropy and mutual information depend continuously on the moments, the continuous mapping theorem yields that the estimators of differential entropy and mutual information introduced in the beginning of this section are consistent.

Theorem 48
.

Let
X
be a continuous RV that has a MGF. Let
\{X_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
be i.i.d. samples drawn according to
P_{X}.
Then, for every
n\in\mathbb{N},
we have the almost-sure convergence

\lim_{m\to\infty}\widehat{h}_{n}\left(\{X_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}\right)=h_{n}(X).

(346)

Furthermore, we have that

h(X)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\lim_{m\to\infty}\widehat{h}_{n}\left(\{X_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}\right)

(347)

where the convergence in
m
is almost-sure convergence.

Proof.

See Appendix
J.1
.
∎

Corollary 49
.

Let
X
be discrete RV with finite support, and
Y
be a continuous RV with a MGF. Let
\{(X_{j},Y_{j})\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
be i.i.d. samples drawn according to
P_{X,Y}.
For every
n\in\mathbb{N},
we have the almost-sure convergence

\lim_{m\to\infty}\widehat{I}_{n}\left(\{(X_{j},Y_{j})\}_{j=1}^{m}\right)=I_{n}(X;Y).

(348)

Furthermore,

I(X;Y)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\widehat{I}_{n}\left(\{(X_{j},Y_{j})\}_{j=1}^{m}\right)

(349)

where the convergence in
m
is almost-sure convergence.

Proof.

See Appendix
J.2
.
∎

11.3
Sample Complexity

When
X
is a continuous RV of bounded support, we may derive the following sample complexity of the estimator of differential entropy in Definition
9
from Hoeffding’s inequality.

Proposition 50
.

Fix a bounded-support continuous RV
X\in L^{2n}(\mathcal{F}).
There is a constant
C=C(X,n)
such that, for all small enough
\varepsilon,\delta>0,
any collection
\mathcal{S}
of i.i.d. samples drawn according to
P_{X}
of size

|\mathcal{S}|>\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log\frac{1}{\delta}

(350)

must satisfy

\mathrm{Pr}\left\{\left|\widehat{h}_{n}(\mathcal{S})-h_{n}(X)\right|<\varepsilon\right\}\geq 1-\delta.

(351)

Proof.

See Appendix
K
.
∎

From Proposition
50
, we may also obtain a sample complexity result for the estimate
\widehat{I}_{n}
in Definition
10
.

Proposition 51
.

Fix a finitely-supported discrete RV
X
and a bounded-support continuous RV
Y\in L^{2n}(\mathcal{F}).
There is a constant
C=C(X,Y,n)
such that, for all small enough
\varepsilon,\delta>0,
any collection
\mathcal{S}
of i.i.d. samples drawn according to
P_{X,Y}
of size

|\mathcal{S}|>\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log\frac{1}{\delta}

(352)

must satisfy

\mathrm{Pr}\left\{\left|\widehat{I}_{n}(\mathcal{S})-I_{n}(X;Y)\right|<\varepsilon\right\}\geq 1-\delta.

(353)

Proof.

See Appendix
K.4
.
∎

11.4
Numerical Results

We compare via synthetic experiments the performance of our estimators
13
13

13

A Python code can be found at
[
49
]
.
against some of the estimators in the literature.

Our proposed estimator for differential entropy is
\widehat{h}_{10},
i.e., given samples
\mathcal{S}
of
X
we estimate
h(X)
by
\widehat{h}_{10}(\mathcal{S})
as given by Definition
9
. We compare this estimator with two estimation methods:
k
-Nearest-Neighbors (
k
-NN), and Kernel Density Estimation (KDE). The
k
-NN-based method we compare against is as provided by the Python package
‘entropy_estimators’

[
50
]
, which we will refer to in this section as KSG. The kernel used for the KDE method is Gaussian, and it is obtained by computing from a set of samples
\{X_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
a kernel
\Phi
via the Python function
‘scipy.stats.gaussian_kde’

[
51
]
; then, the estimate for differential entropy will be
\frac{-1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\log\Phi(X_{j}).
The parameters for the KSG and the KDE estimators are the default parameters, namely,
k=3
for the KSG estimator, and the bandwidth for the KDE estimator is chosen according to Scott’s rule (i.e.,
m^{-1/(d+4)}
for a set of
m
samples of a
d
-RV).

The mutual information is estimated using
\widehat{I_{5}},
i.e., given samples
\mathcal{S}
of
(X,Y)
our estimate for
I(X;Y)
will be
\widehat{I}_{5}(\mathcal{S})
as given by Definition
10
. This estimator is compared against the partitioning estimator and the Mixed KSG estimator
[
40
]
(which is a
k
-NN-based estimator); we utilize the implementation in
[
40
]
for both estimators. In particular, the parameters are fixed throughout, namely, we utilize the parameters used in
[
40
]
(
k=5
for the Mixed KSG, and
8
bins per dimension for the partitioning estimator).

We perform
250
independent trials for each experiment and each fixed sample size, then plot the absolute error as a percentage of the true value (except for the last experiment, where the ground truth is
0,
so we plot the absolute error) against the sample size.

We note that we also performed the mutual information experiments for the Noisy KSG estimator based on the estimator in
[
36
]
(with noise strength
\sigma=0.01
as in
[
40
]
), but its performance was much worse than the other estimators, so we do not include it in the plots.

Experiment 1
.

We estimate the differential entropy of a RV
X
distributed according to Wigner’s semicircle distribution, i.e.,

p_{X}(x):=\frac{2}{\pi}\sqrt{1-x^{2}}\cdot 1_{[-1,1]}(x).

(354)

The ground truth is
h(X)\approx 0.64473
nats. We generate a set
\mathcal{S}
of i.i.d. samples distributed according to
P_{X}.
The size of
\mathcal{S}
ranges from
800
to
4000
in increments of
800,
and for each fixed sample size we independently generate
250
such sets
\mathcal{S}
(so we generate a total of
1250
sets of samples). The differential entropy
h(X)
is estimated by three methods: the moments-based estimator that we propose
\widehat{h}_{10},
the
k
-NN-based estimator implemented in
[
50
]
(which we refer to as the KSG estimator), and the Gaussian KDE estimator. For the proposed estimator, we use
\widehat{h}_{10}(\mathcal{S})
as an estimate for
h(X).
For the KSG estimator, we use the default setting, for which
k=3.
We also use the default setting for the Gaussian KDE estimator; in particular, the bandwidth is chosen according to Scott’s Rule as
m^{-1/(d+4)}
where
m=|\mathcal{S}|
and
d=1
is the dimensionality of
X.
The percentage relative absolute error in the estimation (e.g.,
100\cdot|\widehat{h}_{10}(\mathcal{S})/h(X)-1|
) is plotted against the sample size for the three estimators in Figure
3
. The solid lines in Figure
3
are the means of the errors, i.e., the mean in the
250
independent trials of the percentage relative absolute error for each fixed sample size in
\{800,1600,2400,3200,4000\}.
Via bootstrapping, we infer confidence intervals, which are indicated by the shaded areas around the solid lines in Figure
3
. We see that the proposed estimator outperforms the KSG estimator and the KDE estimator for this experiment.

Figure 3:
Estimation of differential entropy for a semicircle distribution as in Experiment
1
. The vertical axis shows the percentage relative absolute error in the estimation, e.g., for the proposed estimator it is
100\cdot|\widehat{h}_{10}(\mathcal{S})/h(X)-1|
where
\mathcal{S}
is the set of samples and
h(X)\approx 0.64473
nats is the ground truth. The horizontal axis shows
|\mathcal{S}|,
the sample size. The proposed estimator
\widehat{h}_{10}
outperforms the
k
-NN-based estimator (denoted KSG) and the Gaussian KDE estimator for this experiment.

Experiment 2
.

We estimate the differential entropy
h(\bm{X})
of a random vector
\bm{X}=(X_{1},X_{2})^{T}
where
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are i.i.d. distributed according to Wigner’s semicircle distribution, namely,
\bm{X}
has the PDF

p_{\bm{X}}(x,y)=\frac{4}{\pi^{2}}\sqrt{(1-x^{2})(1-y^{2})}\cdot 1_{[-1,1]\times[-1,1]}(x,y).

(355)

The ground truth is
h(\bm{X})\approx 1.28946
nats. The same numerical setup as in Experiment
1
is performed here. The results are plotted in Figure
4
, where we see a similar behavior to the comparison in the 1-dimensional case; in particular, the proposed estimator outperforms the KSG estimator and the KDE estimator for this experiment.

Figure 4:
Estimation of differential entropy for a 2-dimensional semicircle distribution as in Experiment
2
. The proposed estimator
\widehat{h}_{10}
outperforms both the KSG and the KDE estimators for this experiment.

Experiment 3
.

We estimate the differential entropy
h(X)
of a Gaussian mixture
X
whose PDF is given by

p_{X}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{4}\frac{p_{i}}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma_{i}^{2}}}e^{-(x-\mu_{i})^{2}/(2\sigma_{i}^{2})},

(356)

where

\displaystyle\bm{p}

\displaystyle=(0.1,0.2,0.3,0.4)

(357)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}

\displaystyle=(-2,0,1,5)

(358)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}

\displaystyle=(1.5,1,2,1).

(359)

The ground truth is
h(X)\approx 2.34249
nats. The same numerical setup in Experiments
1
and
2
is used here. The results are plotted in Figure
5
. For this experiment, the proposed estimator outperforms the KSG estimator, and it is essentially indistinguishable from the KDE estimator. Note that it is expected that the KDE estimator performs well in this Gaussian mixture experiment, since it is designed specifically to approximate densities by Gaussian mixtures.

Figure 5:
Estimation of differential entropy for a Gaussian mixture as in Experiment
3
. The proposed estimator
\widehat{h}_{10}
outperforms both the KSG and KDE estimators for this experiment. The plot of the KDE estimator’s performance is omitted to avoid cluttering, as it lies just above the line for the proposed estimator but overlaps significantly with its uncertainty region.

Experiment 4
.

We estimate the differential entropy
h(\bm{X})
of a random vector
\bm{X}
that is a mixture of two Gaussians, namely,
\bm{X}
has the PDF

p_{\bm{X}}(\bm{x})=\frac{1}{4\pi\sqrt{\det(\bm{A})}}e^{-(\bm{x}-\boldsymbol{\mu})^{T}\bm{A}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\boldsymbol{\mu})/2}+\frac{1}{4\pi\sqrt{\det(\bm{B})}}e^{-(\bm{x}-\boldsymbol{\nu})^{T}\bm{B}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\boldsymbol{\nu})/2},

(360)

where we have the means
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(-1,-1)^{T}
and
\boldsymbol{\nu}=(1,1)^{T},
and the covariance matrices

\bm{A}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&1/2\\
1/2&1\end{array}\right)

(361)

and
\bm{B}=\bm{I}_{2}.
The ground truth is
h(\bm{X})\approx 3.22406
nats. The same numerical setup as in Experiments
1
-
3
is performed here. The results are plotted in Figure
6
. As in the 1-dimensional case in Experiment
3
, the proposed estimator outperforms the KSG estimator for this experiment. Further, the proposed estimator also outperforms the KDE estimator in this 2-dimensional setting.

Figure 6:
Estimation of differential entropy for a vector Gaussian mixture as in Experiment
4
. The proposed estimator
\widehat{h}_{10}
outperforms both the KSG and KDE estimators for this experiment.

Experiment 5
.

We replicate the mixture-distribution part of the zero-inflated Poissonization experiment of
[
40
]
. In detail, we let
Y\sim\mathrm{Exp}(1),
and let
X=0
with probability
0.15
and
X\sim\mathrm{Pois}(y)
given that
Y=y
with probability
0.85.
The quantity to be estimated is the mutual information
I(X;Y),
and the ground truth is
I(X;Y)\approx 0.25606
nats. We generate a set of i.i.d. samples
\mathcal{S}
according to the distribution
P_{X,Y},
where
\mathcal{S}
has size in
\{800,1600,2400,3200\}.
We estimate
I(X;Y)
via the proposed estimator by
\widehat{I}_{5}(\mathcal{S}),
and we also consider the estimates given by the Mixed KSG estimator and the partitioning estimator, both as implemented in
[
40
]
(including the parameters used therein). This estimation process is repeated independently
250
times. The comparison of estimators’ performance is plotted in Figure
7
. The solid lines indicate the mean percentage relative absolute error, and the shaded areas indicate confidence intervals obtained via bootstrapping. We see in Figure
7
that the proposed estimator outperforms the other considered estimators for this experiment. We also test the affine-transformation invariance property of the proposed estimator. In particular, we consider estimating the mutual information from the scaled samples
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
obtained from
\mathcal{S}
via scaling the
Y
samples by
10^{4},
i.e.,

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}:=\{(A,10^{4}B)\penalty\ ;\penalty\ (A,B)\in\mathcal{S}\}.

(362)

Plotted in Figure
8
is a comparison of the same estimators using the same samples as those used to generate Figure
7
, but where
Y
is processed through this affine transformation. The ground truth stays unchanged, and so do our estimator and the partitioning estimator, but the Mixed KSG estimates change. This experiment illustrates the resiliency of the proposed estimator to affine transformations. In fact, the computed numerical values in the modified setting by the proposed estimator differ by no more than
10^{-15}
nats from those numerically computed in the original setting for each of the
1000
different sets of samples
\mathcal{S}
; in theory, these pairs of values are identical, and the less than
10^{-15}
discrepancy is an artifact of the computer implementation. Finally, we note that although the setup is more general than the assumptions we prove our results under in this paper (as
X
here is not finitely supported), the proposed estimator outperformed the other estimators.

Figure 7:
Percentage relative absolute error vs. sample size for unscaled zero-inflated poissonization in Experiment
5
. The proposed estimator
\widehat{I}_{5}
outperforms both the
k
-NN-based estimator (denoted Mixed KSG) and the partitioning estimator.

Figure 8:
Percentage relative absolute error vs. sample size for the scaled zero-inflated poissonization in Experiment
5
. To generate these plots, we use the same samples that yield the plots in Figure
7
, but we process them through an affine transformation. Specifically, each sample
(A,B)
is replaced with
(A,10^{4}B).
Then the samples are passed to the three estimators. We see that the proposed estimator
\widehat{I}_{5}
is resilient to scaling, i.e., the same performance line in Figure
7
is observed here too. This is in contrast to the performance of the Mixed KSG estimator. The partitioning estimator is resilient to scaling, but its performance is not favorable in this experiment (with above
\%25
relative absolute error).

Experiment 6
.

We test for independence under the following settings. We consider independent
X\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(0.5)
and
Y\sim\mathrm{Unif}([0,2]).
We estimate
I(X;Y),
whose true value is
I(X;Y)=0.
We employ the same estimation procedure as in Experiment
5
. The results are plotted in Figure
9
, which shows that the proposed estimator predicted independence more accurately than the other estimators for the same sample size. Note that in this case the plot shows the absolute error (in nats) rather than the relative absolute error, as the ground truth is zero.

Figure 9:
Absolute error (in nats) vs. sample size for the independence testing in Experiment
6
. The proposed estimator
\widehat{I}_{5}
outperforms the Mixed KSG and the partitioning estimators in this experiment.

Appendix A
A Derivation of Equation (
58
)

Using the notation of
[
52
]
, we have that

C_{r}=\sum_{k=1}^{r}(k-1)!\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{r}{k}_{\geq 2}

(363)

where
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{r}{k}_{\geq 2}
denotes the number of partitions of an
r
-element set into
k
subsets each of which contains at least
2
elements (note that there are
(k-1)!
cyclically-invariant arrangements of
k
parts). The exponential generating function of the sequence
r\mapsto\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{r}{k}_{\geq 2}
is
(e^{x}-1-x)^{k}/k!.
Now, we may write

(e^{x}-1-x)^{k}=\sum_{a+b\leq k}\binom{k}{a,b}(-1)^{k-a}x^{b}\sum_{t\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{(ax)^{t}}{t!}.

(364)

Therefore, the coefficient of
x^{r}
in
(e^{x}-1-x)^{k}/k!
is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r!}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{r}{k}_{\geq 2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a+b\leq k}\frac{(-1)^{k-a}a^{r-b}}{a!b!(k-a-b)!(r-b)!}

(365)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r!}\sum_{b=0}^{k}\binom{r}{b}\sum_{a=0}^{k-b}(-1)^{k-a}\frac{a^{r-b}}{a!(k-a-b)!}

(366)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r!}\sum_{b=0}^{k}\binom{r}{b}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{r-b}{k-b}(-1)^{b},

(367)

which when combined with (
363
) gives (
58
).

Appendix B
Proof of Lemma
2

Assume that
\mathbb{E}\left[|X|^{\deg(p)}\right]=\infty
(so
\deg(p)\geq 1
), and we will show that
\mathbb{E}\left[|p(X)|\right]=\infty
too. Let
k\in[\deg(p)-1]
be the largest integer for which
\mathbb{E}\left[|X|^{k}\right]<\infty,
and write
p(u)=u^{k+1}q(u)+r(u)
for a nonzero polynomial
q
and a remainder
r\in\mathscr{P}_{k}.
By monotonicity of norms,
\mathbb{E}\left[|X|^{j}\right]<\infty
for every
j\in[k].
Hence,
r(X)
is integrable. Therefore, it suffices to prove that
X^{k+1}q(X)
is non-integrable, which we show next.

Consider the set
\mathcal{D}=\left\{u\in\mathbb{R}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ \left|q(u)\right|<|a|\right\}
where
a\neq 0
is the leading coefficient of
q.
If
q
is constant, then
\mathcal{D}
is empty, whereas if
\deg q\geq 1
then
|q(u)|\to\infty
as
|u|\to\infty
implies that
\mathcal{D}
is bounded; in either case, there is an
M\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\mathcal{D}\subset[-M,M].
Now, writing
1=1_{\mathcal{D}}+1_{\mathcal{D}^{c}},
we obtain

\mathbb{E}\left[|X|^{k+1}|q(X)|\right]\geq|a|\penalty\ \mathbb{E}\left[|X|^{k+1}1_{\mathcal{D}^{c}}(X)\right].

(368)

But we also have that

\infty=\mathbb{E}\left[|X|^{k+1}\right]\leq M^{k+1}+\mathbb{E}\left[|X|^{k+1}1_{\mathcal{D}^{c}}(X)\right],

(369)

so
\mathbb{E}\left[|X|^{k+1}1_{\mathcal{D}^{c}}(X)\right]=\infty.
Therefore, inequality (
368
) yields that
\mathbb{E}\left[\left|X^{k+1}q(X)\right|\right]=\infty,
concluding the proof.

Appendix C
Proofs of Section
3
: PMMSE Preliminaries

C.1
Proof of Proposition
4
: Equivalence of PMMSE Definitions

In this proof, we drop the subscript on the norm, so
\|\mathrel{\;\cdot\;}\|
is the norm of
L^{2}(\mathcal{F}).
Let
\{p_{i}(Y)\}_{i\in[d]}
be an orthonormal basis for
\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y).
Then, the orthogonal projection,
E_{n}^{A}[X\mid Y],
of
X
onto
\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y)
is given by

E_{n}^{A}[X\mid Y]=\sum_{i\in[d]}\langle X,p_{i}(Y)\rangle p_{i}(Y),

(370)

with
d=\dim\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y)-1.

We next show that
E_{n}^{A}[X\mid Y]
is the unique closest element in
\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y)
to
X.
Note that
L^{2}(\mathcal{F})
is separable. Extend the orthonormal basis
\{p_{i}(Y)\}_{i\in[d]}
to an orthonormal basis
\{p_{i}(Y)\}_{i\in[d]}\cup\{f_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
for
L^{2}(\mathcal{F}).

Fix an arbitrary
q(Y)\in\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y)
and let
\bm{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
be such that
q(Y)=\sum_{i\in[d]}a_{i}p_{i}(Y).
We may expand
X
as

X=\sum_{i\in[d]}\langle X,p_{i}(Y)\rangle p_{i}(Y)+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\langle X,f_{j}\rangle f_{j}.

(371)

Then, the projection
E_{n}^{A}[X\mid Y]
is at least as close to
X
as
q(Y)
is, because

\displaystyle\left\|X-E_{n}^{A}[X\mid Y]\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|X-\sum_{i\in[d]}\langle X,p_{i}(Y)\rangle p_{i}(Y)\right\|^{2}=\left\|\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\langle X,f_{j}\rangle f_{j}\right\|^{2}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}|\langle X,f_{j}\rangle|^{2}

(372)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in[d]}|\langle X,p_{i}(Y)\rangle-a_{i}|^{2}+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}|\langle X,f_{j}\rangle|^{2}

(373)

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{i\in[d]}(\langle X,p_{i}(Y)\rangle-a_{i})p_{i}(Y)+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\langle X,f_{j}\rangle f_{j}\right\|^{2}=\|X-q(Y)\|^{2}.

(374)

Finally, we show uniqueness. So, assume that
r\in\mathscr{P}_{n}
is such that

\|X-E_{n}^{A}[X\mid Y]\|=\|X-r(Y)\|,

(375)

so this value lower bounds
\|X-p(Y)\|
for any
p(Y)\in\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y).
Note that
(E_{n}^{A}[X\mid Y]+r(Y))/2\in\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y).
Hence,
\|X-(E_{n}^{A}[X\mid Y]+r(Y))/2\|
is lower bounded by both
\|X-E_{n}^{A}[X\mid Y]\|
and
\|X-r(Y)\|.
We utilize the parallelogram law,
\|a+b\|^{2}+\|a-b\|^{2}=2\|a\|^{2}+2\|b\|^{2}
for
a,b\in L^{2}(\mathcal{F}).
Setting
a=X-E_{n}^{A}[X\mid Y]
and
b=X-r(Y)
in the parallelogram law, we have that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\|E_{n}^{A}[X\mid Y]-r(Y)\|^{2}=\|(X-r(Y))-(X-E_{n}^{A}[X\mid Y])\|^{2}

(376)

\displaystyle=2\|X-r(Y)\|^{2}+2\|X-E_{n}^{A}[X\mid Y]\|^{2}-4\|X-(E_{n}^{A}[X\mid Y]+r(Y))/2\|^{2}\leq 0.

(377)

Therefore,
\|E_{n}^{A}[X\mid Y]-r(Y)\|=0,
i.e.,
E_{n}^{A}[X\mid Y]=r(Y).
Hence,
E_{n}^{A}[X\mid Y]
uniquely minimizes
\|X-p(Y)\|
over
p(Y)\in\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y).
Therefore, by definition of
E_{n}^{B}[X\mid Y],
we get that
E_{n}^{B}[X\mid Y]
is well defined and that

E_{n}^{A}[X\mid Y]=E_{n}^{B}[X\mid Y],

(378)

and the proof is complete.

C.2
Proof of Theorem
5
: PMMSE Converges to MMSE

First, we show that the limits hold if
Y
has finite support. Suppose
\mathrm{supp}(Y)=\{y_{j}\}_{j\in[m]}.
Let
q\in\mathscr{P}_{m}
be the polynomial interpolant of
\{\left(y_{j},\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y_{j}]\right)\}_{j\in[m]},
so
q(Y)=\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y].
Then, for every
n\geq m,
we have that
E_{n}[X\mid Y]=q(Y)=\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y].
Hence, the limits (
102
) and (
103
) hold in this case. Thus, we may assume that
|\mathrm{supp}(Y)|=\infty.

By Carleman’s condition, polynomials are dense in
L^{2}(\sigma(Y))

[
5
]
, i.e.,

\overline{\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y)}=L^{2}(\sigma(Y)).

(379)

In particular, the collection of monomials
\{Y^{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a complete set, i.e., if
Z\in L^{2}(\sigma(Y))
satisfies
\langle Z,Y^{k}\rangle=0
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
then it must be that
Z=0.
Further, since
|\mathrm{supp}(Y)|=\infty,
the monomials are linearly independent. Hence, applying Gram-Schmidt, one obtains an orthonormal basis for the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\sigma(Y))
consisting of polynomials
\{q_{k}(Y)\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
q_{k}\in\mathscr{P}_{k}
for each
k\in\mathbb{N}.

Therefore, we may write

\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\langle\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y],q_{k}(Y)\rangle q_{k}(Y)

(380)

where the series converges in the
L^{2}(\sigma(Y))
-norm sense. Further, by the orthogonality principle for
\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]

\langle\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y],q_{k}(Y)\rangle=\langle X,q_{k}(Y)\rangle

(381)

for every
k\in\mathbb{N}.
Now, by the characterization in (
86
) that
E_{n}[X\mid Y]
is the orthogonal projection of
X
onto
\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y),
we know that

E_{n}[X\mid Y]=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\langle X,q_{k}(Y)\rangle q_{k}(Y)

(382)

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
(note that
\{q_{k}(Y)\}_{k\in[n]}
is an orthonormal basis for
\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y)
). Therefore,

\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\langle X,q_{k}(Y)\rangle q_{k}(Y)=\lim_{n\to\infty}E_{n}[X\mid Y],

(383)

i.e., (
102
) holds.

Finally, for (
103
), note that

\displaystyle\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(X\mid Y)

\displaystyle=\|X-E_{n}[X\mid Y]\|_{2}^{2}

(384)

\displaystyle\mathrm{mmse}(X\mid Y)

\displaystyle=\|X-\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\|_{2}^{2}.

(385)

Since both RVs
E_{n}[X\mid Y]
and
\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]
are elements in
L^{2}(\sigma(Y)),
the orthogonality principle of
\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]
implies that
X-\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]
and
E_{n}[X\mid Y]-\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]
are orthogonal. Then, the Pythagorean theorem implies that

\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(X\mid Y)-\mathrm{mmse}(X\mid Y)=\|\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]-E_{n}[X\mid Y]\|_{2}^{2},

(386)

and the limit (
103
) follows from the limit (
102
).

C.3
Proof of Lemma
7
: Invertibility of
\bm{M}_{Y,n}

The matrix
\bm{M}_{Y,n}
is symmetric. We show that it is positive-semidefinite, and that it is positive-definite if and only if
|\mathrm{supp}(Y)|>n.
For any
\bm{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1},
we have the inequality

\displaystyle\bm{d}^{T}\bm{M}_{Y,n}\bm{d}

\displaystyle=\bm{d}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(n)}\left(\bm{Y}^{(n)}\right)^{T}\right]\bm{d}=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{d}^{T}\bm{Y}^{(n)}\left(\bm{Y}^{(n)}\right)^{T}\bm{d}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\bm{d}^{T}\bm{Y}^{(n)}\right|^{2}\right]\geq 0,

(387)

so
\bm{M}_{Y,n}
is positive-semidefinite. Furthermore, the equality case

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\bm{d}^{T}\bm{Y}^{(n)}\right|^{2}\right]=0

(388)

holds if and only if
\left|\bm{d}^{T}\bm{Y}^{(n)}\right|^{2}=0,
and this latter relation holds if and only if
\bm{d}^{T}\bm{Y}^{(n)}=0.
Therefore,
\bm{M}_{Y,n}
is positive-definite if and only if
\bm{d}^{T}\bm{Y}^{(n)}=0
implies
\bm{d}=\mathbf{0},
i.e.,
\bm{Y}^{(n)}
does not lie almost surely in a hyperplane in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.
Finally,
\bm{Y}^{(n)}
lies almost surely in a hyperplane in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
if and only if
|\mathrm{supp}(Y)|\leq n.
Therefore, the desired result that
\bm{M}_{Y,n}
is invertible if and only if
|\mathrm{supp}(Y)|>n
follows.

C.4
Proof of Lemma
8
: An Orthonormal Basis for
\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y)

First, note that Lemma
7
implies that
\bm{M}_{Y,n}
is invertible. To show orthonormality, we show that the expectation of the outer product of the random vector

\bm{V}=\bm{M}_{Y,n}^{-1/2}\bm{Y}^{(n)}

(389)

is the identity matrix. We have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{V}\bm{V}^{T}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{M}_{Y,n}^{-1/2}\bm{Y}^{(n)}\left(\bm{Y}^{(n)}\right)^{T}\left(\bm{M}_{Y,n}^{-1/2}\right)^{T}\right]=\bm{M}_{Y,n}^{-1/2}\bm{M}_{Y,n}\left(\bm{M}_{Y,n}^{-1/2}\right)^{T}=\bm{I}_{n+1},

(390)

where we have used that (see (
49
))

\bm{M}_{Y,n}^{1/2}\left(\bm{M}_{Y,n}^{1/2}\right)^{T}=\bm{M}_{Y,n}

(391)

and (see (
50
))

\bm{M}_{Y,n}^{-1/2}=\left(\bm{M}_{Y,n}^{1/2}\right)^{-1}.

(392)

Hence, the entries of the vector
\bm{M}_{Y,n}^{-1/2}\bm{Y}^{(n)}
form an orthonormal subset of
\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y).
Since we have that
\mathrm{span}(\{1,\cdots,Y^{n}\})=\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y),
and
\bm{M}_{Y,n}^{-1/2}
is invertible, we conclude that the entries of
\bm{M}_{Y,n}^{-1/2}\bm{Y}^{(n)}
also span
\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y),
which completes the proof.

C.5
A Second Proof for Theorem
6

By assumption on
Y,
Lemma
8
yields invertibility of
\bm{M}_{Y,n}.
Denote

\bm{c}_{X,Y,n}:=\bm{M}_{Y,n}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[X\bm{Y}^{(n)}\right],

(393)

and we will show that strict convexity of the function
g:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to[0,\infty)
defined by

g(\bm{d})=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(X-\bm{d}^{T}\bm{Y}^{(n)}\right)^{2}\right]

implies that
\bm{c}_{X,Y,n}
is its unique minimizer. For any
\bm{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1},
linearity of expectation implies that the gradient of
g
is

\nabla g(\bm{d})=\left(\mathbb{E}\left[2Y^{k}\left(\bm{d}^{T}\bm{Y}^{(n)}-X\right)\right]\right)_{0\leq k\leq n},

so the Hessian of
g
is the constant
2\bm{M}_{Y,n}.
As
\bm{M}_{Y,n}
is positive-definite,
g
is strictly convex. As
\nabla g(\bm{d})={\bf 0}
is equivalent to
\bm{M}_{Y,n}\bm{d}=\mathbb{E}\left[X\bm{Y}^{(n)}\right],
i.e., to
\bm{d}=\bm{c}_{X,Y,n},
the desired result follows.

Appendix D
Proofs of Section
4
: Basic PMMSE Properties

D.1
Proof of Proposition
9
: PMMSE and Affine Transformations

Set
U=Y+\beta.
For any
\bm{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1},

X+\alpha-\bm{c}^{T}\bm{U}^{(n)}=X-(\bm{M}\bm{c}-\alpha\bm{e}_{1})^{T}\bm{Y}^{(n)},

(394)

where we define the matrix

\bm{M}:=\left(\beta^{i-j}\binom{i}{j}\right)_{(i,j)\in[n]^{2}},

(395)

and we set
\beta^{i-j}\binom{i}{j}=0
when
j>i
and
\beta^{0}\binom{i}{i}=1
when
\beta=0.
Then
\bm{M}
is lower-triangular with an all-
1
diagonal, so the inverse
\bm{M}^{-1}
exists. Thus, the mapping
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
defined by
\bm{c}\mapsto\bm{M}\bm{c}-\alpha\bm{e}_{1}
is invertible (where
\bm{d}\mapsto\bm{M}^{-1}(\bm{d}+\alpha\bm{e}_{1})
is the inverse mapping). Therefore, the following two subsets of
L^{2}(\mathcal{F})
are equal

\left\{X+\alpha-\bm{c}^{T}\bm{U}^{(n)}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ \bm{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\right\}=\left\{X-\bm{d}^{T}\bm{Y}^{(n)}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ \bm{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\right\}.

(396)

Then, by the definition of the PMMSE, equality (
128
) holds.

Equation (
129
) may be treated similarly. First, note that the case
\alpha=0
is immediate, because then
\alpha X=0\in\mathscr{P}_{n}(\beta Y)
implies
\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(\alpha X\mid\beta Y)=0.
Assume
\alpha\neq 0.
Setting
V=\beta Y,
one has that for any
\bm{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}

\alpha X-\bm{c}^{T}\bm{V}^{(n)}=\alpha\left(X-(\bm{L}\bm{c})^{T}\bm{Y}^{(n)}\right)

(397)

where we define the invertible matrix

\bm{L}:=\mathrm{diag}\left(\left(\beta^{k}/\alpha\right)_{k\in[n]}\right).

(398)

As
\bm{c}\mapsto\bm{L}\bm{c}
is a bijection of
\mathbb{R}^{n+1},
the definition of the PMMSE yields equation (
129
).

D.2
Proof of Lemma
11
: PMMSE Operator Properties

Let
\bm{e}_{0},\bm{e}_{1},\cdots,\bm{e}_{n}
denote the standard basis vectors for
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.
For
(i)
, we first note that

\bm{M}_{Z,n}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Z}^{(n)}\right]=\bm{e}_{0},

(399)

because
\mathbb{E}[\bm{Z}^{(n)}]=\bm{M}_{Z,n}\bm{e}_{0}.
By the formula for
E_{n}[X\mid Z]
in Theorem
6

E_{n}[X\mid Z]=\mathbb{E}\left[X\bm{Z}^{(n)}\right]^{T}\bm{M}_{Z,n}^{-1}\,\bm{Z}^{(n)}.

(400)

Taking the expectation and using (
399
),

\mathbb{E}\left[E_{n}[X\mid Z]\right]=\mathbb{E}[X].

(401)

For
(ii)
, we generalize this approach and note that

\bm{M}_{Z,n}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[Z^{j}\bm{Z}^{(n)}\right]=\bm{e}_{j}.

(402)

for each
j\in[n].
Then, for any monomial
Z^{j}
with
j\in[n]
we have from (
400
) and (
402
) that

\mathbb{E}\left[E_{n}[X\mid Z]Z^{j}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[X\bm{Z}^{(n)}\right]^{T}\bm{e}_{j}=\mathbb{E}[XZ^{j}].

(403)

In other words, for all
j\in[n],

\mathbb{E}\left[(X-E_{n}[X\mid Z])Z^{j}\right]=0.

(404)

By linearity of expectation, we conclude that for any polynomial
p\in\mathscr{P}_{n}

\mathbb{E}[(X-E_{n}[X\mid Z])p(Z)]=0.

(405)

Properties
(iii)
-
(vi)
follow immediately by Proposition
10
. Alternatively, the linearity in
(iii)
follows from linearity of expectation in view of the formula for
E_{n}[\mathrel{\;\cdot\;}\mid Z]
in (
400
), the contractivity in
(iv)
follows since
0\leq\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(X\mid Y)=\|X\|_{2}^{2}-\|E_{n}[X\mid Y]\|_{2}^{2},
the idempotence in
(v)
follows directly from the fact that
E_{n}[X\mid Z]\in\mathscr{P}_{n}(Z),
and the self-adjointness in
(vi)
can be verified via formula (
400
).

If
X
and
Z
are independent, then

\mathbb{E}\left[X\bm{Z}^{(n)}\right]=\mathbb{E}[X]\penalty\ \mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Z}^{(n)}\right].

(406)

Therefore, from
\bm{M}_{Z,n}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Z}^{(n)}\right]=\bm{e}_{0}
in (
399
), and in view of formula (
400
) for
E_{n}[X\mid Z],
we have that

E_{n}[X\mid Z]=\mathbb{E}[X]\bm{e}_{0}^{T}\bm{Z}^{(n)}=\mathbb{E}[X],

(407)

so
(vii)
follows.

Finally, for
(viii)
, assume that
X
—
Y
—
Z
is a Markov chain. Then, for every
j\in[n]

\mathbb{E}\left[XZ^{j}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[XZ^{j}\mid Y\right]\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[X\mid Y\right]\mathbb{E}\left[Z^{j}\mid Y\right]\right].

(408)

Further, we have that

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[X\mid Y\right]\mathbb{E}\left[Z^{j}\mid Y\right]\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[X\mid Y\right]Z^{j}\right]

(409)

by the orthogonality property for
\mathbb{E}[\mathrel{\;\cdot\;}\mid Y]
:

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[X\mid Y\right]\left(Z^{j}-\mathbb{E}\left[Z^{j}\mid Y\right]\right)\right]=0.

(410)

Combining (
408
) and (
409
), we obtain that for every
j\in[n]

\mathbb{E}\left[XZ^{j}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[X\mid Y\right]Z^{j}\right].

(411)

Multiplying (
411
) on the right by
\bm{M}_{Z,n}^{-1}\bm{Z}^{(n)},
the formula for
E_{n}[\mathrel{\;\cdot\;}\mid Z]
(see (
400
)) implies that

E_{n}\left[X\mid Z\right]=E_{n}\left[\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\mid Z\right],

(412)

as desired.

D.3
Proof of Proposition
12
: PMMSE for Symmetric RVs

We may assume that
X
and
Z
are symmetric around
0,
since
E_{m}[X+a\mid X+Z+b]=a+E_{m}[X\mid X+Z]
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
a,b\in\mathbb{R}.
Then,
\mathbb{E}[X^{j}]=\mathbb{E}[Z^{j}]=0
for every odd
j\in\mathbb{N}.
Set
Y=X+Z
and
n=2k.
Then,
\mathbb{E}[Y^{j}]=0
for every odd
j\in\mathbb{N},
and
\mathbb{E}[XY^{\ell}]=0
for every even
\ell\in\mathbb{N}.
Then, the coefficient of
Y^{n}
in
E_{n}[X\mid Y]
is

\frac{1}{\det\bm{M}_{Y,n}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in[n]\\
\ell\penalty\ \text{odd}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[XY^{\ell}\right]\left[\bm{M}_{Y,n}^{-1}\right]_{\ell,n},

(413)

where
\left[\bm{M}_{Y,n}^{-1}\right]_{\ell,n}
denotes the
(\ell,n)
-th entry of
\bm{M}_{Y,n}^{-1}.
Fix an odd
\ell\in[n].
Let
T_{n}^{(\ell,n)}
denote the set of permutations of
[n]
that send
\ell
to
n.
We have that

\left[\bm{M}_{Y,n}^{-1}\right]_{\ell,n}=-\sum_{\pi\in T_{n}^{(\ell,n)}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\prod_{r\in[n]\setminus\{\ell\}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{r+\pi(r)}\right].

(414)

We have that, for every
\pi\in T_{n}^{(\ell,n)},

\sum_{r\in[n]\setminus\{\ell\}}r+\pi(r)=n(n+1)-\ell-n,
which is odd. Therefore, for at least one
r\in[n]\setminus\{\ell\},
the integer
r+\pi(r)
is odd. Hence,
\mathbb{E}[Y^{r+\pi(r)}]=0,
implying that
\left[\bm{M}_{Y,n}^{-1}\right]_{\ell,n}=0.
As this is true for every odd
\ell\in[n],
we conclude that the coefficient of
Y^{n}
in
E_{n}[X\mid Y]
is
0,
i.e., (
143
) holds. Equation (
144
) follows from (
143
) as
\mathrm{pmmse}_{m}(X\mid Y)=\|X-E_{m}[X\mid Y]\|_{2}^{2}
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}.

Appendix E
Proofs of Section
5

E.1
Proof of Theorem
17
: Uniform Convergence of the PMMSE

We start the proof by obtaining from Proposition
5
pointwise convergence. Let
N\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
be independent of
X.
We shall verify the assumptions in Proposition
5
on
\sqrt{t}X+N
for fixed
t\geq 0
:
i)
As
N
is continuous and
X
is independent of
N
we must have
|\mathrm{supp}(\sqrt{t}X+N)|=\infty,
and
ii)
The MGF of
\sqrt{t}X+N
exists (it is the product of the MGFs of
\sqrt{t}X
and
N
) and this implies that
\sqrt{t}X+N
satisfies Carleman’s condition
[
5
]
. Hence, by Proposition
5
, we get that for every
t\geq 0

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(X,t)=\mathrm{mmse}(X,t).

(415)

Now, we show that the convergence is uniform.

Set, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in[0,\infty),

g_{n}(t):=\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(X,t)-\mathrm{mmse}(X,t)

(416)

for short. We will show that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,\infty)}g_{n}(t)=0,

(417)

which is the uniform convergence in (
160
).

The limit (
415
) says that

\lim_{n\to\infty}g_{n}(t)=0

(418)

for every fixed
t\geq 0.
In addition, the asymptotics given in Corollary
15
imply that for each fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}

\lim_{t\to\infty}g_{n}(t)=0.

(419)

By definition of the PMMSE as the minimum over sets of increasing size (in
n
), for each fixed
t\geq 0
the sequence
\{\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(X,t)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is decreasing. So
\{g_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a pointwise decreasing sequence of functions (i.e.,
g_{1}(t)\geq g_{2}(t)\geq\cdots
for each fixed
t\geq 0
). Note that the
g_{n}
are nonnegative. We finish the proof via Cantor’s intersection theorem.

Fix
\varepsilon>0.
For each
n\in\mathbb{N},
let
C_{\varepsilon,n}=g^{-1}_{n}([\varepsilon,\infty)),
where
g^{-1}
denotes the set-theoretic inverse. As
\{g_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is decreasing,
C_{\varepsilon,1}\supseteq C_{\varepsilon,2}\supseteq\cdots
is decreasing too. As each
g_{n}
is continuous, each
C_{\varepsilon,n}
is closed. Further,
\lim_{t\to\infty}g_{1}(t)=0
implies that
C_{\varepsilon,1}
is bounded, hence each
C_{\varepsilon,n}
is bounded. Thus, each
C_{\varepsilon,n}
is compact. But, the intersection
\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}C_{\varepsilon,n}
is empty, for if
t_{0}
were in the intersection then
\liminf_{n\to\infty}g_{n}(t_{0})\geq\varepsilon
violating that
\lim_{n\to\infty}g_{n}(t_{0})=0.
Hence, by Cantor’s intersection theorem, it must be that the
C_{\varepsilon,n}
are eventually empty, i.e., there is an
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\sup_{t\in[0,\infty)}g_{n}(t)\leq\varepsilon
for every
n>m.
This is precisely the uniform convergence in (
417
), and the proof is complete.

E.2
Proof of Lemma
19

The integer
i+\pi(i)
is odd if and only if
i
and
\pi(i)
have opposite parities. Thus, the desired result follows from the following more general characterization. For any partition
[n]=A\cup B,
the cardinality of the set

I:=\left\{i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}\;;\;(i,\pi(i))\in(A\times B)\cup(B\times A)\right\}

(420)

is even. The desired result follows by letting
A
and
B
be even and odd integers, respectively, in
[n].
Now, we show that the general characterization holds.

Let
A_{\pi}\subset A
denote the subset of elements of
A
that get mapped by
\pi
into
B,
i.e.,

A_{\pi}:=\{i\in A\;;\;\pi(i)\in B\},

(421)

and define
B_{\pi}
similarly. Then,
I=A_{\pi}\cup B_{\pi}
is a partition. As
|A_{\pi}|=|B_{\pi}|,
the desired result follows.

E.3
Proof of Lemma
21

For each
(i,j)\in[n]^{2}
let the subset
T_{n}^{(i,j)}\subset\mathcal{S}_{[n]}
denote the collection of permutations sending
i
to
j,
i.e.,

T_{n}^{(i,j)}:=\left\{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ \pi(i)=j\right\}.

(422)

We define, for each
(i,j)\in[n]^{2},
the cofactor functions
c_{X,n}^{(i,j)}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
and the products
d_{X,n}^{(i,j)}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle c_{X,n}^{(i,j)}(t)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\pi\in T_{n}^{(i,j)}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\prod_{k\neq i}\left(\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N,n}\right)_{k,\pi(k)},

(423)

\displaystyle d_{X,n}^{(i,j)}(t)

\displaystyle:=v_{X,i}(t)\penalty\ c_{X,n}^{(i,j)}(t)\penalty\ v_{X,j}(t).

(424)

Here,
\left(\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N,n}\right)_{a,b}
is the
(a,b)
-th entry of
\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N,n},
i.e.,

\left(\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N,n}\right)_{a,b}=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sqrt{t}X+N\right)^{a+b}\right].

(425)

Note that
c_{X,n}^{(i,j)}(t)
is the
(i,j)
-th cofactor of
\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N,n}.
The cofactor matrix
\bm{C}_{X,n}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times(n+1)}
of
t\mapsto\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N,n}
is given by

\displaystyle\bm{C}_{X,n}

\displaystyle:=\left(c_{X,n}^{(i,j)}\right)_{(i,j)\in[n]^{2}}.

(426)

We define the function
D_{X,t}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
by

D_{X,n}:=\bm{v}_{X,n}^{T}\bm{C}_{X,n}\bm{v}_{X,n}.

(427)

We have the following two relations. First,
D_{X,n}
is the sum of the
d_{X,n}^{(i,j)}

D_{X,n}(t)=\sum_{(i,j)\in[n]^{2}}d_{X,n}^{(i,j)}(t).

(428)

Second, by Cramer’s rule, and because symmetry of the matrix
\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N,n}
implies that its cofactor is equal to its adjugate, we have the formula

\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N,n}^{-1}=\frac{1}{\det\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N,n}}\penalty\ \bm{C}_{X,n}.

(429)

Therefore, we obtain

F_{X,n}(t)=\frac{D_{X,n}(t)}{\det\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N,n}}=\frac{\sum_{(i,j)\in[n]^{2}}d_{X,n}^{(i,j)}(t)}{\det\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N,n}}.

(430)

Hence, it suffices to study the
d_{X,n}^{(i,j)}.

We start with a characterization of the cofactors
c_{X,n}^{(i,j)}.
Namely, we show that if
i+j
is even then
c_{X,n}^{(i,j)}(t)
is a polynomial in
t,
and if
i+j
is odd then
\sqrt{t}c_{X,n}^{(i,j)}(t)
is a polynomial in
t.
If
i+j
is even, then

c_{X,n}^{(i,j)}(t)=\sum_{\pi\in T_{n}^{(i,j)}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\penalty\ t^{\delta(\pi)/2}\quad\quad\prod_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in[n]\\
k+\pi(k)\;\text{odd}\end{subarray}}}\quad o_{1,X,k+\pi(k)}(t)\quad\quad\prod_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}r\in[n],\penalty\ r\neq i\\
r+\pi(r)\;\text{even}\;\end{subarray}}}\quad e_{1,X,r+\pi(r)}(t),

(431)

whereas if
i+j
is odd then

c_{X,n}^{(i,j)}(t)=\sum_{\pi\in T_{n}^{(i,j)}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\penalty\ t^{\frac{\delta(\pi)-1}{2}}\quad\quad\prod_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in[n],\penalty\ k\neq i\\
k+\pi(k)\;\text{odd}\end{subarray}}}\quad o_{1,X,k+\pi(k)}(t)\quad\quad\prod_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}r\in[n]\\
r+\pi(r)\;\text{even}\end{subarray}}}\quad e_{1,X,r+\pi(r)}(t).

(432)

Thus, evenness of
\delta(\pi)
for each
\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}
implies that each
c_{X,n}^{(i,j)}(t)
is a polynomial when
i+j
is even and that each
\sqrt{t}c_{X,n}^{(i,j)}(t)
is a polynomial when
i+j
is odd. Further, the degree of
c_{X,n}^{(i,j)}
for even
i+j
is upper bounded by

\frac{\delta(\pi)}{2}+\sum_{k+\pi(k)\;\text{odd}}\frac{k+\pi(k)-1}{2}+\sum_{r+\pi(r)\;\text{even}\,;\,r\neq i}\frac{r+\pi(r)}{2}=\frac{n(n+1)}{2}-\frac{i+j}{2},

(433)

whereas the degree of
\sqrt{t}c_{X,n}^{(i,j)}
and for odd
i+j
is upper bounded by

\frac{\delta(\pi)}{2}+\sum_{k+\pi(k)\;\text{odd}\,;\,k\neq i}\frac{k+\pi(k)-1}{2}+\sum_{r+\pi(r)\;\text{even}}\frac{r+\pi(r)}{2}=\frac{n(n+1)}{2}-\frac{i+j-1}{2}.

(434)

We note that both upper bounds are equal to

\frac{n(n+1)}{2}-\left\lfloor\frac{i+j}{2}\right\rfloor.

(435)

Finally, considering the terms of highest order, we see that the term

\sum_{\pi\in T_{n}^{(i,j)}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\prod_{k\in[n]\setminus\{i\}}\mathcal{X}_{k+\pi(k)}

(436)

is the coefficient of
t^{\frac{n(n+1)}{2}-\left\lfloor\frac{i+j}{2}\right\rfloor}
in
c_{X,n}^{(i,j)}
when
i+j
is even and in
\sqrt{t}c_{X,n}^{(i,j)}
when
i+j
is odd.

Now, to show that
D_{X,n}
is a polynomial, it suffices to check that each
d_{X,n}^{(i,j)}
is. We consider separately the parity of
i+j
and build upon the characterization of
c_{X,n}^{(i,j)}.
If
i+j
is even, so
i
and
j
have the same parity, then

\mathbb{E}\left[X\left(\sqrt{t}X+N\right)^{i}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[X\left(\sqrt{t}X+N\right)^{j}\right]

is a polynomial in
t
of degree at most
(i+j)/2
with the coefficient of
t^{(i+j)/2}
being
\mathcal{X}_{i+1}\mathcal{X}_{j+1}.
If
i+j
is odd, so
i
and
j
have different parities, then

t^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}\left[X\left(\sqrt{t}X+N\right)^{i}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[X\left(\sqrt{t}X+N\right)^{j}\right]

is a polynomial in
t
of degree at most
(i+j-1)/2
with the coefficient of
t^{(i+j-1)/2}
being
\mathcal{X}_{i+1}\mathcal{X}_{j+1}.

Thus, from the characterization of
c_{X,n}^{(i,j)},
regardless of the parity of
i+j
we obtain that
d_{X,n}^{(i,j)}(t)
is a polynomial in
t
of degree at most
n(n+1)/2
with the coefficient of
t^{n(n+1)/2}
being

\mathcal{X}_{i+1}\mathcal{X}_{j+1}\sum_{\pi\in T_{n}^{(i,j)}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\prod_{k\in[n]\setminus\{i\}}\mathcal{X}_{k+\pi(k)}.

(437)

Summing over all
(i,j)\in[n]^{2},
we obtain that the coefficient of
t^{n(n+1)/2}
in
D_{X,n}(t)
is

\sum_{(i,j)\in[n]^{2}}\sum_{\pi\in T_{n}^{(i,j)}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\mathcal{X}_{i+1}\mathcal{X}_{j+1}\prod_{k\in[n]\setminus\{i\}}\mathcal{X}_{k+\pi(k)}.

(438)

The proof is completed by noting that, for each
i\in[n],
we have a partition

\mathcal{S}_{[n]}=\bigcup_{j\in[n]}T_{n}^{(i,j)}.

(439)

E.4
Proof of Lemma
22

The formulas for
a_{X}^{n,0}
and
b_{X}^{n,0}
follow by setting
t=0
in (
213
) and (
214
). Indeed, if
N\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
is independent of
X,
then

F_{X,n}(0)=\mathcal{X}_{1}^{2}\penalty\ \mathbb{E}\left[\bm{N}^{(n)}\right]^{T}\bm{M}_{N,n}^{-1}\penalty\ \mathbb{E}\left[\bm{N}^{(n)}\right]=\mathcal{X}_{1}^{2}

(440)

because
\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{N}^{(n)}\right]
is the leftmost column of
\bm{M}_{N,n}.
Therefore,

a_{X}^{n,0}=\sigma_{X}^{2}\det\bm{M}_{N,n}=\sigma_{X}^{2}b_{X}^{n,0}.

(441)

Further, by direct computation or using the connection between Hankel matrices and orthogonal polynomials
[
53
, Appendix A]
along with the fact that the probabilist’s Hermite polynomials
q_{k}
satisfy the recurrence
xq_{k}(x)=q_{k+1}(x)+kq_{k-1}(x),
it follows that
\det\bm{M}_{N,n}=\prod_{k=1}^{n}k!=G(n+2)
where
G
is the Barnes
G
-function. Equation (
219
) for
b_{X}^{n,d_{n}}
is proved in Lemma
20
. To simplify the proof of formula (
218
) for
b_{X}^{n,1},
we first show the shift-invariance stated in (
221
).

Fix
s\in\mathbb{R}.
For any i.i.d. RVs
Z,Z_{0},\cdots,Z_{n},
we have that (see, e.g.,
[
53
, Appendix A]
)

\det\bm{M}_{Z,n}=\frac{1}{(n+1)!}\penalty\ \mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{0\leq i<j\leq n}(Z_{i}-Z_{j})^{2}\right].

(442)

From equation (
442
), since
(Z_{i}+s)-(Z_{j}+s)=Z_{i}-Z_{j},
we obtain that

\det\bm{M}_{Z+s,n}=\det\bm{M}_{Z,n}.

(443)

Let
N\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
be independent of
X.
Then, for every
t\in[0,\infty),
considering
Z=\sqrt{t}X+N
in (
443
), we obtain

\det\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}(X+s)+N,n}=\det\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N,n}.

(444)

As both sides of (
444
) are polynomials in
t,
we obtain that
b_{X+s}^{n,j}=b_{X}^{n,j}
for every
j\in[d_{n}].
Since we also have
\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(X+s,t)=\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(X,t),
it follows that

t\mapsto\sum_{j\in[d_{n}]}a_{X}^{n,j}t^{j}=\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(X,t)\sum_{j\in[d_{n}]}b_{X}^{n,j}t^{j}

(445)

is also invariant under shifting
X,
so we also obtain
a_{X+s}^{n,j}=a_{X}^{n,j}.

By the shift-invariance of
b_{X}^{n,1},
we may assume that
\mathcal{X}_{1}=0
(so
\mathcal{X}_{2}=\sigma_{X}^{2}
). Now, as each entry in
\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N,n}
is a polynomial in
\sqrt{t},
we see that we may drop any term of order
(\sqrt{t})^{3}
or above for the sake of finding
b_{X}^{n,1}
(which is the coefficient of
t
in
\det\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N,n}
). In other words,

b_{X}^{n,1}=\det\left(\binom{i+j}{2}\sigma_{X}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[N^{i+j-2}\right]t+\mathbb{E}\left[N^{i+j}\right]\right)_{(i,j)\in[n]^{2}}\hskip-1.0pt.

(446)

By Leibniz’s formula, we conclude

b_{X}^{n,1}=\sigma_{X}^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}\\
k\in[n]\end{subarray}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\binom{k+\pi(k)}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[N^{k+\pi(k)-2}\right]\prod_{i\in[n]\setminus\{k\}}\mathbb{E}\left[N^{i+\pi(i)}\right].

(447)

But, for any non-negative integer
m

\binom{m}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[N^{m-2}\right]=\frac{m}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[N^{m}\right].

(448)

Therefore, (
447
) simplifies to

b_{X}^{n,1}=\frac{\sigma_{X}^{2}}{2}\hskip-5.0pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}\\
k\in[n]\end{subarray}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)(k+\pi(k))\prod_{i\in[n]}\mathbb{E}\left[N^{i+\pi(i)}\right].

(449)

Evaluating the summation over
k
for each fixed
\pi,
we obtain that

b_{X}^{n,1}=\binom{n+1}{2}\sigma_{X}^{2}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\prod_{i\in[n]}\mathbb{E}\left[N^{i+\pi(i)}\right].

(450)

Finally, by Leibniz’s formula for
\det\bm{M}_{N,n},
we obtain that

b_{X}^{n,1}=\binom{n+1}{2}\sigma_{X}^{2}\det\bm{M}_{N,n},

(451)

as desired.

E.5
Formulas for
a_{X}^{n,j}

We have (see (

\left(\mathcal{X}_{2}-F_{X,n}(t)\right)\det\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N,n}=\sum_{j\in[d_{n}]}a_{X}^{n,j}t^{j}.

(452)

We obtain from (

)

\mathcal{X}_{2}\det\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N,n}=\sum_{j\in[d_{n}]}\mathcal{X}_{2}b_{X}^{n,j}t^{j}.

(453)

The function

F_{X,n}(t)\det\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N,n}=\bm{v}_{X,n}(t)^{T}\bm{C}_{\sqrt{t}X+N,n}\bm{v}_{X,n}(t),

(454)

\bm{u}^{T}\bm{R}\bm{u}\hskip 5.69054pt=\sum_{(i,\pi)\in[n]\times\mathcal{S}_{[n]}}(-1)^{i+\pi(i)}\hskip 2.84526pt\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)u_{i}u_{\pi(i)}\prod_{r\in[n]\setminus\{i\}}h_{r+\pi(r)}.

Applying (

(456)

226
) from (
225
), expanding the powers and expectations we obtain from (

455
) that

\displaystyle F_{X,n}(t)\det\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N,n}\hskip 8.53581pt=\hskip 28.45274pt\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}(i,\pi)\in[n]\times\mathcal{S}_{[n]}\\

k_{r}\in[r+\pi(r)],\penalty\ \forall r\in[n]\setminus\{i\}\end{subarray}}}\hskip 14.22636ptt^{(w+z+s_{i}(\bm{k}))/2}\gamma_{i,\pi,\bm{k},w,z}\mathcal{X}_{w+1}\mathcal{X}_{z+1}\prod_{r\in[n]\setminus\{i\}}\mathcal{X}_{k_{r}},

where

14
14

14

An alternative proof of Lemma

\displaystyle\gamma_{i,\pi,\bm{k},w,z}=(-1)^{i+\pi(i)}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\binom{i}{w}\binom{\pi(i)}{z}\mathbb{E}[N^{i-w}]\mathbb{E}[N^{\pi(i)-z}]\prod_{r\in[n]\setminus\{i\}}\binom{r+\pi(r)}{k_{r}}\mathbb{E}[N^{r+\pi(r)-k_{r}}],

(458)

\displaystyle F_{X,n}(t)\det\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N,n}\hskip 8.53581pt=\hskip 5.69054pt\sum_{j\in[d_{n}]}\hskip 2.84526ptt^{j}\hskip 36.98857pt\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}(i,\pi)\in[n]\times\mathcal{S}_{[n]}\\

\left(\mathcal{X}_{2}-F_{X,n}(t)\right)\det\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N,n}=\sum_{j\in[d_{n}]}a_{X}^{n,j}t^{j},

(461)

we obtain that

\displaystyle a_{X}^{n,j}\hskip 8.53581pt=\hskip 28.45274pt\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}\\
k_{r}\in[r+\pi(r)],\penalty\ \forall r\in[n]\\
k_{0}+\cdots+k_{n}=2j\end{subarray}}}\hskip 22.76219pt\beta_{\pi;k_{0},\cdots,k_{n}}\mathcal{X}_{2}\mathcal{X}_{k_{0}}\cdots\mathcal{X}_{k_{n}}\hskip 8.53581pt-\hskip 28.45274pt\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}(i,\pi)\in[n]\times\mathcal{S}_{[n]}\\
(w,z)\in[i]\times[\pi(i)]\\
k_{r}\in[r+\pi(r)],\penalty\ \forall r\in[n]\setminus\{i\}\\
w+z+s_{i}(\bm{k})=2j\end{subarray}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\gamma_{i,\pi,\bm{k},w,z}\mathcal{X}_{w+1}\mathcal{X}_{z+1}\prod_{r\in[n]\setminus\{i\}}\mathcal{X}_{k_{r}}.

(462)

From (
462
), we obtain

a_{X}^{n,j}\in H_{2j+2,\min(n,2j)+2,\tau_{n}(j)}(X)

(463)

\tau_{n}(j)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}2&\text{if }j=0,\\

E.6
Proof of Lemma
23

Fix
\varepsilon>0.
Let
W
be a discrete RV with finite support such that
\mathbb{E}\left[W^{k}\right]=\mu_{k}
for each
k\in[m],
which exists by the solution to the truncated Hamburger moment problem
[
47
, Theorem 3.1]
. Set
A=\mathrm{supp}(W),
and let
\{q_{a}\}_{a\in A}\subset[0,1]
be such that
p_{W}=\sum_{a\in A}q_{a}\delta_{a}
is the PMF of
W.
Then, for each
k\in[m],
we have that

\sum_{a\in A}q_{a}a^{k}=\mu_{k}.

(465)

For each
s>0,

a\in A,
and
k\in[m],
we have that

\int_{a-s}^{a+s}\frac{z^{k}}{2s}\,dz=\frac{(a+s)^{k+1}-(a-s)^{k+1}}{2s(k+1)}.

(466)

Further, we have the limit

\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}\frac{(a+s)^{k+1}-(a-s)^{k+1}}{2s(k+1)}=a^{k}.

(467)

Therefore, there exist constants
\eta_{a,k}>0,
for
(a,k)\in A\times[m],
such that
0<s\leq\eta_{a,k}
implies

\left|\frac{(a+s)^{k+1}-(a-s)^{k+1}}{2s(k+1)}-a^{k}\right|<\varepsilon.

(468)

Let
\eta=\min_{(a,k)\in A\times[m]}\eta_{a,k}
and consider the continuous RV
Z
whose PDF is given by

p_{Z}(z)=\sum_{a\in A}\frac{q_{a}}{2\eta}1_{[a-\eta,a+\eta]}(z).

(469)

Thus, for each
k\in[m],

\mathbb{E}\left[Z^{k}\right]=\sum_{a\in A}q_{a}\cdot\frac{(a+\eta)^{k+1}-(a-\eta)^{k+1}}{2\eta(k+1)}\in\left(\sum_{a\in A}q_{a}(a^{k}-\varepsilon),\sum_{a\in A}q_{a}(a^{k}+\varepsilon)\right)=\left(\mu_{k}-\varepsilon,\mu_{k}+\varepsilon\right),

(470)

which follow by (
466
), (
468
), and (
465
), respectively.

E.7
Proof of Proposition
24

We proceed by induction on
m.
The case
m=1
follows because then by assumption on
p
we have that
p(k)=0
for every positive integer
k
as can be seen by taking
X\sim\mathcal{N}(k,1),
but the only polynomial with infinitely many zeros is the zero polynomial. Now, assume that the statement of the proposition holds for every polynomial in
m-1
variables, where
m\geq 2.

Fix a polynomial
p
in
m
variables, and assume that
p|_{\mathcal{C}^{m}}=0.
Regarding
p
as a polynomial in one of the variables with coefficients being polynomials in the remaining
m-1
variables, we may write

p(u_{1},\cdots,u_{m})=\sum_{j\in[d]}p_{j}(u_{1},\cdots,u_{m-1})u_{m}^{j},

(471)

for some polynomials
p_{0},\cdots,p_{d}
in
m-1
variables, where
d
is the total degree of
p.
We show that
p=0
identically by showing that each
p_{j}
vanishes on
\mathcal{C}_{m-1}
and using the induction hypothesis to obtain
p_{j}=0
identically.

Fix
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{m-1})\in\mathcal{C}_{m-1}.
Let
\mu_{m}
be a variable, and set
\ell=\lfloor m/2\rfloor.
We have that
\ell=(m-1)/2
if
m
is odd, and
\ell=m/2
if
m
is even. Set
\bm{H}=(\mu_{i+j})_{(i,j)\in[\ell]^{2}}.
If
m
is even, then
\det\bm{H}=\alpha\mu_{m}+\beta
for some constants
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
determined by
\boldsymbol{\mu},
with
\alpha=\det(\mu_{i+j})_{(i,j)\in[\ell-1]^{2}}>0.
In the case
m
is even, we set
t=-\beta/\alpha,
and in the case
m
is odd, we set
t=0.
Then,
\bm{H}
is positive definite whenever
\mu_{m}>t.

For each integer
k\geq 1
and real
\varepsilon>0,
Lemma
23
yields a RV
X_{k,\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{R}_{m}
satisfying

\delta_{k,j}(\varepsilon):=\mathbb{E}[X_{k,\varepsilon}^{j}]-\mu_{j}\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)

(472)

for each
j\in\{1,\cdots,m-1\}
and

\delta_{k,m}(\varepsilon):=\mathbb{E}[X_{k,\varepsilon}^{m}]-(t+k)\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon).

(473)

Then, by assumption on
p,
for every
\varepsilon>0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},

\sum_{j\in[d]}p_{j}(\mu_{1}+\delta_{k,1}(\varepsilon),\cdots,\mu_{m-1}+\delta_{k,m-1}(\varepsilon))(t+k+\delta_{k,m}(\varepsilon))^{j}=0.

(474)

Taking the limit
\varepsilon\to 0^{+},
we deduce that

\sum_{j\in[d]}p_{j}(\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{m-1})(t+k)^{j}=0.

(475)

Considering (
475
) as a univariate polynomial in
k,
we see that its vanishing at infinitely many values of
k
implies that

p_{j}(\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{m-1})=0

(476)

for every
j\in[d].
This holds for every
(\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{m-1})\in\mathcal{C}_{m-1},
i.e., the premise of the proposition applies to each
p_{j}
(namely, for every
X\in\mathcal{R}_{m-1}
we have
p_{j}(\mathbb{E}[X],\cdots,\mathbb{E}[X^{m-1}])=0
). By the induction hypothesis, we obtain
p_{j}=0,
as polynomials, for every
j\in[d].
Therefore,
p=0,
and the proof is complete.

Appendix F
Proofs of Section
6

F.1
Proof of Proposition
27
: Conditional Expectation Derivatives

Recall that the conditional expectation can be expressed as

\mathbb{E}[Z\mid Y=y]=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[Ze^{-(X-y)^{2}/2}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-(X-y)^{2}/2}\right]}

(477)

for any RV
Z
for which
Ze^{-(X-y)^{2}/2}
is integrable. This formula applies for both
Z=X
and
Z=(X-\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y])^{k},
where
(y,k)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{N},
because they are polynomials in
X
and the map
x\mapsto q(x)e^{-(x-y)^{2}/2}
is bounded for any polynomial
q.

Differentiating (
477
) for
Z=X
and rearranging terms, we obtain

\frac{d}{dy}\penalty\ \mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(X-\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y])^{2}e^{-(X-y)^{2}/2}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-(X-y)^{2}/2}\right]},

(478)

i.e.,
f^{\prime}=g_{2}.
Note that
g_{0}\equiv 1
and
g_{1}\equiv 0.
Differentiating
g_{r}
for
r\geq 1,
we obtain that

g_{r}^{\prime}=g_{r+1}-rg_{2}g_{r-1}.

(479)

We apply successive differentiation to
f^{\prime}=g_{2}
and recover patterns by utilizing (
479
) at each step.

From
f^{\prime}=g_{2}
and (
479
), we infer the first few derivatives

f^{(2)}=g_{3},\penalty\ f^{(3)}=g_{4}-3g_{2}^{2},\penalty\ f^{(4)}=g_{5}-10g_{2}g_{3}.

(480)

We see a homogeneity in (
480
), namely,
f^{(r-1)}
is an integer linear combination of terms of the form
g_{i_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots g_{i_{\ell}}^{\alpha_{\ell}}
with
i_{1}\alpha_{1}+\cdots+i_{\ell}\alpha_{\ell}=r.
This homogeneity can be shown to hold for a general
r
by induction, which we show next. For most of the remainder of the proof, we forget the numerical values of the
f^{(k)}
and the
g_{r}^{(k)}
and only treat them as symbols satisfying
f^{\prime}=g_{2}
and
g_{r}^{\prime}=g_{r+1}-rg_{2}g_{r-1}
that respect rules of differentiation and which commute.

We call
\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}i_{j}\alpha_{j}
the
weighted degree
of any nonzero integer multiple of
g_{i_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots g_{i_{\ell}}^{\alpha_{\ell}}.
This is a well-defined degree because it is invariant to the way the product is arranged. We also say that a sum is of weighted degree
r
if each summand is of weighted degree
r.
To prove the claim of homogeneity, i.e., that
f^{(r-1)}
is of weighted degree
r,
we differentiate and apply the relation in (
479
) to a generic term
g_{i_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots g_{i_{\ell}}^{\alpha_{\ell}}
whose weighted degree is
r.
We have the derivative

\left(g_{i_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots g_{i_{\ell}}^{\alpha_{\ell}}\right)^{\prime}=\left(g_{i_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\right)^{\prime}\cdots g_{i_{\ell}}^{\alpha_{\ell}}+\cdots+g_{i_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\left(g_{i_{\ell}}^{\alpha_{\ell}}\right)^{\prime}.

(481)

From (
479
), for integers
i,\alpha\geq 1,

\left(g_{i}^{\alpha}\right)^{\prime}=\alpha g_{i}^{\alpha-1}g_{i+1}-\alpha ig_{2}g_{i-1}g_{i}^{\alpha-1}.

(482)

Therefore, the derivative of
g_{i}^{\alpha}
has weighted degree
i\alpha+1.
In other words, differentiation increased the weighted degree of
g_{i}^{\alpha}
by
1.
From (
481
), then, we see that the weighted degree of
\left(g_{i_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots g_{i_{\ell}}^{\alpha_{\ell}}\right)^{\prime}
is
r+1.
Since
f^{\prime}=g_{2}
is of weighted degree
2,
induction and linearity of differentiation yield that
f^{(r-1)}
is of weighted degree
r
for each
r\geq 2.

Now, we fix the way we are writing products of the
g_{i}.
We ignore explicitly writing
g_{0}
and
g_{1},
collect identical terms into an exponent, and write lower indices first. One way to keep this notation is via integer partitions. Consider the “homogeneous” sets

G_{r}:=\left\{\sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi_{r}}\beta_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\bm{g}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ \beta_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{Z}\penalty\ \text{for each}\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi_{r}\right\}.

(483)

The homogeneity property for the derivatives of
f
can be written as
f^{(r-1)}\in G_{r}
for each
r\geq 2.

Next, we investigate the exact integer coefficients
h_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
in the expression of the derivatives of
f
in terms of the
\bm{g}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}.
Homogeneity of the derivatives of
f
says that we may write each
f^{(r-1)},

r\geq 2,
as an integer linear combination of
\{\bm{g}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi_{r}}.
One way to obtain such a representation is via repeated differentiation of
f^{\prime}=g_{2},
applying the relation (
482
), and discarding any term that is a multiple of
g_{1}.
Applying these steps, we arrive at representations

f^{(r-1)}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi_{r}}h_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\bm{g}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ c_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{Z}.

(484)

The terms
\bm{g}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
that appear upon differentiating a term
\bm{g}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
can be described as follows. For
(\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{\ell})=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi_{r},
we call
\lambda_{2}
the leading term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}.
Consider for a tuple
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi_{r}
the following two sets of tuples
\tau_{+}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),\tau_{-}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\subset\Pi_{r+1}
:

•

The set
\tau_{+}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
consists of all tuples obtainable from
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
via replacing a pair
(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{i+1})
with
(\lambda_{i}-1,\lambda_{i+1}+1)
(so, necessarily
\lambda_{i}\geq 1
) while keeping all other entries unchanged;

•

The set
\tau_{-}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
consists of all tuples obtainable from
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
via replacing a pair
(\lambda_{i-1},\lambda_{i}),
for which
i\geq 3,
with the pair
(\lambda_{i-1}+1,\lambda_{i}-1)
(so, necessarily
\lambda_{i}\geq 1
) and additionally increasing the leading term by
1
while keeping all other terms unchanged.

For example, if
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(0,5,0,1)\in\Pi_{20}
then

\tau_{+}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\{(0,4,1,1),(0,5,0,0,1)\}\subset\Pi_{21}

(485)

and

\tau_{-}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\{(2,4,0,1),(1,5,1)\}\subset\Pi_{21}.

(486)

The relation (
482
) yields, in view of

\left(g_{2}^{\lambda_{2}}\cdots g_{\ell}^{\lambda_{\ell}}\right)^{\prime}=\left(g_{2}^{\lambda_{2}}\right)^{\prime}\cdots g_{\ell}^{\lambda_{\ell}}+\cdots+g_{2}^{\lambda_{2}}\cdots\left(g_{\ell}^{\lambda_{\ell}}\right)^{\prime},

(487)

that

\left(\bm{g}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\right)^{\prime}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\tau_{+}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}a_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}}\bm{g}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}-\sum_{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\tau_{-}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}b_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}}\bm{g}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}

(488)

for some positive integers
a_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}}
and
b_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}},
which we describe next. Finding
a_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}}
and
b_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}}
can be straightforwardly done from (
482
) in view of (
487
). If
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\tau_{+}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),
say

(\nu_{i},\nu_{i+1})=(\lambda_{i}-1,\lambda_{i+1}+1),

(489)

then
a_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}}=\lambda_{i}.
If
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\tau_{-}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),
say

(\nu_{i-1},\nu_{i})=(\lambda_{i-1}+1,\lambda_{i}-1),

(490)

then
b_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}}=i\lambda_{i}.
In our example of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(0,5,0,1),
we get

\displaystyle a_{(0,5,0,1),(0,4,1,1)}

\displaystyle=5

(491)

\displaystyle a_{(0,5,0,1),(0,5,0,0,1)}

\displaystyle=1,

(492)

whereas

\displaystyle b_{(0,5,0,1),(2,4,0,1)}

\displaystyle=15

(493)

\displaystyle b_{(0,5,0,1),(1,5,1)}

\displaystyle=5.

(494)

Note that the two sets
\tau_{+}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
and
\tau_{-}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
are disjoint because, e.g., the sum of entries of a tuple in
\tau_{+}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
is the same as that for
\boldsymbol{\lambda},
whereas the sum of entries of a tuple in
\tau_{-}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
is one more than that for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}.

We next describe how to use what we have shown thus far to deduce a recurrence relation for the
h_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}.
Let
\theta
be a process inverting
\tau,
i.e., define for
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\Pi_{r+1}
the two sets

\theta_{+}(\boldsymbol{\nu}):=\left\{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi_{r}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\nu}\in\tau_{+}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right\}

(495)

and

\theta_{-}(\boldsymbol{\nu}):=\left\{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi_{r}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\nu}\in\tau_{-}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right\}

(496)

The two sets
\theta_{+}(\boldsymbol{\nu})
and
\theta_{-}(\boldsymbol{\nu})
are disjoint because the two sets
\tau_{+}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
and
\tau_{-}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
are disjoint for each fixed
\boldsymbol{\lambda}.
Recall our process for defining
h_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
: we start with
f^{\prime}=g_{2},
so
h_{(1)}=1
; we successively differentiate
f^{\prime}=g_{2}
; after each differentiation, we use (
482
) and (
487
) (recall that we have the understanding
g_{i}^{0}=1
); we discard any ensuing multiple of
g_{1}
; after
r-2
differentiations, we get an equation
f^{(r-1)}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi_{r}}h_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\bm{g}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}},
which we take to be the definition of the
h_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}.
The point here is that it could be that
f^{(r-1)}
is representable as an integer linear combination of the
\bm{g}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
in more than one way, which can only be verified after the numerical values for the
g_{i}
are taken into account, but we are not doing that: our approach treats the
g_{i}
as symbols following the laid out rules. Now, we look at one of the steps of this procedure, starting at differentiating
f^{(r-1)}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi_{r}}h_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\bm{g}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}},
so
f^{(r)}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi_{r}}h_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\left(\bm{g}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\right)^{\prime}.
Replacing
(\bm{g}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})^{\prime}
via (
488
),

f^{(r)}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi_{r}}\left(\sum_{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\tau_{+}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}a_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}}\bm{g}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}-\sum_{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\tau_{-}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}b_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}}\bm{g}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right).

(497)

Exchanging the order of summations (for which we use
\theta
),

f^{(r)}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\Pi_{r+1}}\left(\sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\theta_{+}(\boldsymbol{\nu})}h_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}a_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}}-\sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\theta_{-}(\boldsymbol{\nu})}h_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}b_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right)\bm{g}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}.

(498)

Therefore, by definition of the
h_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}},
we have the recurrence: for each
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\Pi_{r+1}

h_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\theta_{+}(\boldsymbol{\nu})}h_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}a_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}}-\sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\theta_{-}(\boldsymbol{\nu})}h_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}b_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}},\quad h_{(1)}=1.

(499)

One instance of this recurrence is, e.g.,

h_{(2,1)}=3h_{(3)}-4h_{(1,0,1)}-6h_{(0,2)}.

(500)

Now, we show that the recurrence in (
499
) also generates
e_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
as defined in (
57
). For
(\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{\ell})=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi_{r},
denote
\sigma(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\lambda_{2}+\cdots+\lambda_{\ell}.
If
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\tau_{+}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
then
\sigma(\boldsymbol{\nu})=\sigma(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),
and if
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\tau_{-}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
then
\sigma(\boldsymbol{\nu})=\sigma(\boldsymbol{\lambda})+1.
Therefore,
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\theta_{+}(\boldsymbol{\nu})
implies
\sigma(\boldsymbol{\nu})=\sigma(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\theta_{-}(\boldsymbol{\nu})
implies
\sigma(\boldsymbol{\nu})=\sigma(\boldsymbol{\lambda})+1.
Multiplying (
499
) by
(-1)^{\sigma(\boldsymbol{\nu})-1}
yields the equivalent recurrence

t_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\theta_{+}(\boldsymbol{\nu})}t_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}a_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}}+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\theta_{-}(\boldsymbol{\nu})}t_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}b_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}},\quad t_{(1)}=1,

(501)

where
t_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}:=(-1)^{\sigma(\boldsymbol{\lambda})-1}h_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}.
We show that
c_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=(-1)^{\sigma(\boldsymbol{\lambda})-1}e_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
(see (
57
)) satisfies this recurrence, which is equivalent to
e_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
satisfying the recurrence (
499
). Clearly,
c_{(1)}=1,
so consider
c_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
for
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\Pi_{r}
with
r\geq 3.

Consider labelled elements
s_{1},s_{2},\cdots,
and let
S_{k}=\{s_{1},\cdots,s_{k}\}
for each
k\geq 2.
For any
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi_{k},
let
\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
be the set of arrangements of cyclically-invariant set-partitions of
S_{k}
according to
\boldsymbol{\lambda},
so
|\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}|=c_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}.
Now, fix
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\Pi_{r+1},
and we will build
\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
from the
\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
where
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
ranges over
\theta_{+}(\boldsymbol{\nu})\cup\theta_{-}(\boldsymbol{\nu}).
Consider first
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\theta_{+}(\boldsymbol{\nu}),
where a partition in
\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
is constructed from a partition in
\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
by appending
s_{r+1}
to one of the parts of the latter partition. Note that adding
s_{r+1}
to two distinct partitions of
S_{r}
cannot produce the same partition of
S_{r+1}
; indeed, just removing
s_{r+1}
shows that that is impossible. Now, let
i
be the unique index such that
(\nu_{i},\nu_{i+1})=(\lambda_{i}-1,\lambda_{i+1}+1).
Then, a partition
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
of
S_{r+1}
is induced by a partition
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
of
S_{r}
if and only if
s_{r+1}
is added to a part in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of size
i,
of which there are exactly
\lambda_{i}=a_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}}.
Therefore, we get a contribution of
\sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\theta_{+}(\boldsymbol{\nu})}c_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}a_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}}
towards
c_{\boldsymbol{\nu}},
which is the first part in (
501
).

For the second part,
\sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\theta_{-}(\boldsymbol{\nu})}c_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}b_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}},
we consider the remaining ways of generating a partition in
\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
from a partition according to some
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\theta_{-}(\boldsymbol{\nu}).
In this case,
s_{r+1}
is not appended to an existing part, but it is used to create a new part of size
2.
Thus, we need to also move an element
s_{j},

1\leq j\leq r,
from a part of size at least
3
to be combined with
s_{r+1}
to create a new part of size
2.
It is also clear in this case that such a procedure applied to two distinct partitions in
\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
cannot produce the same partition in
\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}.
Let
i
be the unique index for which
(\nu_{i-1},\nu_{i})=(\lambda_{i-1}+1,\lambda_{i}-1).
There are
\lambda_{i}
parts to choose from, and
i
elements to choose from once a part is chosen, so there are a total of
i\lambda_{i}=b_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}}
ways to generate a partition in
\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
from a partition in
\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}.
This gives the second sum in (
501
), and we conclude that

c_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\theta_{+}(\boldsymbol{\nu})}c_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}a_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}}+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\theta_{-}(\boldsymbol{\nu})}c_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}b_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}}.

(502)

Therefore, the
c_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
and the
t_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
satisfy the same recurrence, which takes the form: for
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\Pi_{r+1}
there are integers
\{d_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}}\}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi_{r}}
such that

u_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi_{r}}d_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}}u_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}

(503)

with the initial condition
u_{(1)}=1.
Then, we can induct on
r
to conclude that the
c_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
and the
t_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
are the same sequence. Since
\Pi_{2}=\{(1)\},
we see that
c_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=t_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
for every
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi_{2}.
Suppose
r\geq 2
is such that
c_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=t_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
for every
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi_{r}.
Hence, for every
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\Pi_{r+1},
we have that

\sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi_{r}}d_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}}c_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi_{r}}d_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}}t_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}.

(504)

Since both sequences
c_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
and
t_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
satisfy the recurrence (
503
), we obtain from (
504
) that
c_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=t_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
for every
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\Pi_{r+1}.
Therefore, we obtain by induction that
c_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=t_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
for every
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi_{r}
for every
r,
as desired.

F.2
Proof of Theorem
29

Fix
p\in\mathscr{D},
suppose
X\sim p,
and write
Y=X+N
and
p_{Y}=e^{-Q}.
First, we note that
Q^{\prime}(y)
is equal to
\mathbb{E}[N\mid Y=y].

Lemma 52
.

Fix a random variable
X
and let
Y=X+N
where
N\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
is independent of
X.
Writing
p_{Y}=e^{-Q},
we have that
Q^{\prime}(y)=\mathbb{E}[N\mid Y=y].

Proof.

We have that
p_{Y}(y)=\mathbb{E}[e^{-(y-X)^{2}/2}]/\sqrt{2\pi}.
Differentiating this equation, we obtain that
p_{Y}^{\prime}(y)=\mathbb{E}[(X-y)e^{-(y-X)^{2}/2}]/\sqrt{2\pi},
where the exchange of differentiation and integration is warranted since
t\mapsto te^{-t^{2}/2}
is bounded. Now,
Q=-\log p_{Y},
so
Q^{\prime}=-p_{Y}^{\prime}/p_{Y},
i.e.,

Q^{\prime}(y)=y-\frac{\mathbb{E}[Xe^{-(y-X)^{2}/2}]}{\mathbb{E}[e^{-(y-X)^{2}/2}]}=y-\mathbb{E}\left[X\mid Y=y\right].

(505)

The proof is completed by substituting
X=Y-N.

∎

In view of Lemma
52
, that
p
is even and non-increasing over
[0,\infty)\cap\mathrm{supp}(p)
imply that
Q
satisfies conditions
(1)
–
(4)
of Definition
5
. It remains to show that property
(5)
holds. To this end, we show that if
\mathrm{supp}(p)\subset[-M,M]
and
\lambda=M+2,
then for every
y>M+4
we have that

1<\frac{M^{2}+5M+8}{2(M+2)}\leq\frac{Q^{\prime}(\lambda y)}{Q^{\prime}(y)}\leq\frac{M^{2}+7M+8}{4}.

(506)

First, since
Q^{\prime}(y)=y-\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]
(see (
505
)), we have the bounds
y-M\leq Q^{\prime}(y)\leq y+M
for every
y\in\mathbb{R}.
Therefore,
y>M
and
\lambda>1
imply that

\frac{\lambda y-M}{y+M}\leq\frac{Q^{\prime}(\lambda y)}{Q^{\prime}(y)}\leq\frac{\lambda y+M}{y-M}.

(507)

Further, since
y>M+4
and
\lambda=M+2,
we have

\frac{M^{2}+5M+8}{2(M+2)}<\lambda-\frac{M(M+3)}{y+M}=\frac{\lambda y-M}{y+M}

(508)

and

\frac{\lambda y+M}{y-M}=\lambda+\frac{M(M+3)}{y-M}\leq\frac{M^{2}+7M+8}{4}.

(509)

The fact that
1<\frac{M^{2}+5M+8}{2(M+2)}
follows since the discriminant of
M^{2}+3M+4
is
-7<0.
Therefore,
p_{Y}
is a Freud weight.

F.3
Proof of Inequality (
245
)

By Lemma
52
,

Q^{\prime}(y)=\mathbb{E}[N\mid Y=y]=y-\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y].

(510)

Therefore
X\leq M
implies that, for any constant
z\geq 0,
we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{ztQ^{\prime}(zt)}{\sqrt{1-t^{2}}}\,dt

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{4}z^{2}-z\int_{0}^{1}\hskip-4.0pt\frac{t}{\sqrt{1-t^{2}}}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[Xe^{-(X-zt)^{2}/2}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-(X-zt)^{2}/2}\right]}dt

(511)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\pi}{4}z^{2}-Mz.

(512)

We have
\pi z^{2}/4-Mz>n
for
z=(2M+\sqrt{2})\sqrt{n}.
Since
y\mapsto yQ^{\prime}(y)
is strictly increasing over
(0,\infty)
(condition
(3)
of Definition
5
), we conclude that
a_{n}(Q)\leq(2M+\sqrt{2})\sqrt{n}.

Appendix G
Proof of Proposition
33
:
h_{n}
Under Affine Transformations

Fix
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
and let
N\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
be independent of
Y.
From equation (
128
) in Proposition
9
, for any fixed
t\geq 0

\displaystyle\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(\alpha Y+\beta,t)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(\alpha Y+\beta\mid\sqrt{t}(\alpha Y+\beta)+N)

(513)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(\alpha Y+\beta\mid\sqrt{\alpha^{2}t}Y+\beta\sqrt{t}+N)

(514)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(\alpha Y\mid\sqrt{\alpha^{2}t}Y+N).

(515)

Also, from equation (
129
) in Proposition
9
,

\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(\alpha Y\mid\sqrt{\alpha^{2}t}Y+N)=\alpha^{2}\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(Y\mid\sqrt{\alpha^{2}t}Y+N)=\alpha^{2}\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(Y,\alpha^{2}t).

(516)

Combining these two relations, we obtain

\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(\alpha Y+\beta,t)=\alpha^{2}\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(Y,\alpha^{2}t).

(517)

Therefore, by definition of
h_{n}
(see equation (
254
))

\displaystyle h_{n}(\alpha Y+\beta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(\alpha Y+\beta,t)-\frac{1}{2\pi e+t}\,dt

(518)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha^{2}\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(Y,\alpha^{2}t)-\frac{1}{2\pi e+t}\,dt

(519)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(Y,\alpha^{2}t)-\frac{1}{2\pi e\alpha^{2}+\alpha^{2}t}\right)\alpha^{2}dt.

(520)

Performing the substitution
u=\alpha^{2}t,

h_{n}(\alpha Y+\beta)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(Y,u)-\frac{1}{2\pi e\alpha^{2}+u}\,du.

(521)

Finally, from (
251
) (with
a=1/(2\pi e\alpha^{2})
and
b=1/(2\pi e)
), we obtain

\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2\pi e\alpha^{2}+u}-\frac{1}{2\pi e+u}\,du=\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}=-\log|\alpha|.

(522)

Note that the integrand in the right hand side of (
521
) and the integrand in the left hand side of (
522
) are both absolutely integrable over
(0,\infty).
Therefore, adding (
521
) and (
522
), and utilizing the definition of
h_{n}
in (
254
) again, we obtain

h_{n}(\alpha Y+\beta)-\log|\alpha|=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{pmmse}_{n}(Y,u)-\frac{1}{2\pi e+u}\,du=h_{n}(Y),

(523)

and the proof is complete.

Appendix H
Proofs of Section
10

H.1
Proof of Lemma
38

Fix
X,
and let
W
be its orthogonal projection onto the closed subspace
\mathcal{V}.
Consider the symmetric matrix
\bm{M}=\mathbb{E}[\bm{V}\bm{V}^{T}].
Since
\bm{c}^{T}\bm{M}\bm{c}=\mathbb{E}[|\bm{c}^{T}\bm{V}|^{2}]
for each
\bm{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1},
linear independence of the
V_{j}
implies that
\bm{M}
is positive-definite. Let
\bm{M}^{1/2}
be the unique lower-triangular matrix with positive diagonal such that
\bm{M}^{1/2}\left(\bm{M}^{1/2}\right)^{T}=\bm{M},
and denote its inverse by
\bm{M}^{-1/2}.
Let
(U_{0},\cdots,U_{n})^{T}=\bm{U}=\bm{M}^{-1/2}\bm{V}.
Then,
\{U_{j}\}_{j\in[n]}\subset\mathcal{V}
and
\mathbb{E}[\bm{U}\bm{U}^{T}]=\bm{I}_{n+1}.
Therefore,
\{U_{j}\}_{j\in[n]}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{V}
; indeed, it is the output of Gram-Schmidt orthonormalization on
\{V_{j}\}_{j\in[n]}.
Then, because
W
is the orthogonal projection of
X
onto
\mathcal{V},
we can express
W
as

W=\sum_{j\in[n]}\mathbb{E}[XU_{j}]\penalty\ U_{j}=\mathbb{E}[X\bm{U}^{T}]\bm{U}.

(524)

Plugging the defining formulas of
\bm{U}
and
\bm{M}
into (
524
),

W=\mathbb{E}\left[X\bm{V}\right]^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{V}\bm{V}^{T}\right]^{-1}\bm{V}.

(525)

Finally, being the orthogonal projection of
X
onto
\mathcal{V},

W
is the unique closest element in
\mathcal{V}
to
X
; hence, equation (
284
) follows.

H.2
Proof of Corollary
40

Fix
q\geq 1.
For each
j,
Carleman’s condition on
Y_{j}
yields that the set of polynomials in
Y_{j},
i.e.,
\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y_{j}),
is dense in
L^{2q}(\sigma(Y_{j})).
Therefore, by Theorem
39
,

\overline{\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathscr{P}_{n,m}(\bm{Y})}=L^{q}(\sigma(\bm{Y})).

(526)

Now, fix
(f_{1},\cdots,f_{m})^{T}=\bm{f}\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{m},\sigma(\bm{Y})).
For each
j,

f_{j}\in L^{q}(\sigma(\bm{Y})).
Hence, there is a sequence
\{g_{j,n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathscr{P}_{n,m}(\bm{Y})
such that
f_{j}=\lim_{n\to\infty}g_{j,n}
in
L^{q}(\sigma(\bm{Y}))
-norm. Set
\bm{g}_{n}=(g_{1,n},\cdots,g_{m,n})^{T},
and note that
\bm{g}_{n}\in\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{m}(\bm{Y}).
By definition of the norm in
L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{m},\sigma(\bm{Y})),
we deduce

\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\bm{f}-\bm{g}_{n}\|_{q}^{q}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|f_{j}-g_{j,n}\|_{q}^{q}=0,

(527)

and the desired denseness result follows.

H.3
Proof of Theorem
41

Since the
Y_{j}
do not satisfy a polynomial relation, the matrix
\bm{M}_{\bm{Y},n}
is invertible for each
n\in\mathbb{N}.
Further, the entries of
\bm{Y}^{(n,m)}
are linearly independent for each
n.
Then, by Lemma
38
, equation (
301
) follows, i.e.,
E_{n}[\bm{X}\mid\bm{Y}]
