is the
\ell
-RV whose
k
-th entry is
\mathbb{E}\left[X_{k}\bm{Y}^{(n,m)}\right]^{T}\bm{M}_{\bm{Y},n}^{-1}\bm{Y}^{(n,m)}.
By Corollary
40
, since each
Y_{j}
satisfies Carleman’s condition, the set of vectors of polynomials
\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{m}(\bm{Y})
is dense in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{m},\sigma(\bm{Y})).
In particular,
\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathscr{P}_{n,m}(\bm{Y})
is dense in
L^{2}(\sigma(\bm{Y})).
By Theorem
37
, we have the
L^{2}(\sigma(\bm{Y}))
limits

\mathbb{E}[X_{k}\mid\bm{Y}]=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{k}\bm{Y}^{(n,m)}\right]^{T}\bm{M}_{\bm{Y},n}^{-1}\bm{Y}^{(n,m)}

(528)

for each
k\in\{1,\cdots,\ell\}.
We conclude that
E_{n}[\bm{X}\mid\bm{Y}]\to\mathbb{E}[\bm{X}\mid\bm{Y}]
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\ell},\sigma(\bm{Y})),
as desired.

H.4
Proof of Proposition
42

Set
\bm{Y}=(Y_{1},Y_{2})^{T}.
Equation (
306
) is straightforward: since
E_{n}[X\mid Y_{1}]\in\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y_{1})\subset\mathscr{P}_{n,2}(\bm{Y}),
the projection of
E_{n}[X\mid Y_{1}]
onto
\mathscr{P}_{n,2}(\bm{Y})
is
E_{n}[X\mid Y_{1}]
again. Equation (
307
) also follows by an orthogonal projection argument. There is a unique representation
X=p_{1,2}+p_{1,2}^{\perp}
for
(p_{1,2},p_{1,2}^{\perp})\in\mathscr{P}_{n,2}(\bm{Y})\times\mathscr{P}_{n,2}(\bm{Y})^{\perp}.
There is also a unique representation
p_{1,2}=q_{2}+q_{2}^{\perp}
for
(q_{2},q_{2}^{\perp})\in\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y_{2})\times\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y_{2})^{\perp}.
The projection of
X
onto
\mathscr{P}_{n,2}(\bm{Y})
is
p_{1,2},
whose projection onto
\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y_{2})
is
q_{2},
i.e.,

E_{n}\left[E_{n}[X\mid Y_{1},Y_{2}]\mid Y_{2}\right]=q_{2}.

(529)

Furthermore, we have the representation
X=q_{2}+(q_{2}^{\perp}+p_{1,2}^{\perp}),
for which
(q_{2},q_{2}^{\perp}+p_{1,2}^{\perp})\in\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y_{2})\times\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y_{2})^{\perp}.
Hence, the projection of
X
onto
\mathscr{P}_{n}(Y_{2})
is
q_{2}
too, i.e.,

E_{n}[X\mid Y_{2}]=q_{2}.

(530)

From (
529
) and (
530
) we get (
307
). Equation (
307
) can also be deduced from the formula of
W:=\mathbb{E}[X\mid\bm{Y}].
Denote
\bm{Y}_{2}^{(n)}=(1,Y_{2},\cdots,Y_{2}^{n})^{T}.
We have that

W=\mathbb{E}\left[X\bm{Y}^{(n,2)}\right]^{T}\bm{M}_{\bm{Y},n}^{-1}\bm{Y}^{(n,2)}

(531)

and

E_{n}[W\mid Y_{2}]=\mathbb{E}\left[W\bm{Y}_{2}^{(n)}\right]^{T}\bm{M}_{Y_{2},n}^{-1}\bm{Y}_{2}^{(n)}.

(532)

For
k\in[n],
let
\delta(k)\in\left[\binom{n+2}{2}-1\right]
be the index of the entry in
\bm{Y}^{(n,2)}
that equals
Y_{2}^{k}.
Then,

\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{2}^{k}\bm{Y}^{(n,2)}\right]=\bm{M}_{\bm{Y},n}\bm{e}_{\delta(k)},

(533)

where
\bm{e}_{0},\cdots,\bm{e}_{\binom{n+2}{2}-1}
are the standard basis vectors of
\mathbb{R}^{\binom{n+2}{2}}.
Therefore, plugging (
531
) into (
532
), we obtain

E_{n}[W\mid Y_{2}]=\mathbb{E}\left[X\bm{Y}_{2}^{(n)}\right]^{T}\bm{M}_{Y_{2},n}^{-1}\bm{Y}_{2}^{(n)},

(534)

which is just
E_{n}[X\mid Y_{2}],
as desired.

Appendix I
Multidimensional MMSE Dimension (Theorem
43
)

In this proof, we will denote the Euclidean norm in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
by the unsubscripted
\|\mathrel{\;\cdot\;}\|,
so if
\bm{N}=(N_{1},\cdots,N_{m})^{T}
then
\|\bm{N}\|=\sqrt{N_{1}^{2}+\cdots+N_{m}^{m}}
is a RV.

Our proof is a straightforward extension of the proof for the one-dimensional case given in
[
32
]
. In particular, we use the concept of approximations to the identity.

Definition 11
.

The set of functions
\{K_{\delta}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}\}_{\delta>0}
is called
an approximation to the identity
if

(i)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}K_{\delta}(\bm{y})\,d\bm{y}=A
for each
\delta>0,

(ii)

|K_{\delta}(\bm{y})|\leq B\delta^{-m}
for each
\delta>0,
and
\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},

(iii)

|K_{\delta}(\bm{y})|\leq B\delta/\|\bm{y}\|^{m+1}
for each
\delta>0
and
\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\{0\},

where
A
and
B
are constants that are independent of
\delta
and
\bm{y}.

We apply the following theorem
[
45
, Theorem 2.1]
stating that approximations to the identity closely approximate an identity operation under convolution. Recall that a point
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
is called a Lebesgue point of a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
if

\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\mu_{L}(B_{r}(\bm{x}))}\int_{B_{r}(\bm{x})}|f(\bm{y})-f(\bm{x})|\,d\bm{y}=0,

(535)

where
B_{r}(\bm{x})\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
is the open ball around
\bm{x}
of radius
r,
and
\mu_{L}
is the Lebesgue measure in
\mathbb{R}^{m}.

Theorem 53
.

If
\{K_{\delta}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}\}_{\delta>0}
is an approximation to the identity with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}K_{\delta}(\bm{y})\,d\bm{y}=A
for every
\delta>0
(see Definition
11
), then for every
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
we have that

\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}(K_{\delta}\ast f)(\bm{x})=A\cdot f(\bm{x})

(536)

at each Lebesgue point
\bm{x}
of
f.

We use the following auxiliary result showing that the Lebesgue set is Borel.

Lemma 54
.

The set of Lebesgue points of an integrable Borel function is Borel.

Proof.

Fix a Borel function
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m}),
denote the set of its Lebesgue points by
\mathcal{L},
and define the function
F:\mathbb{R}^{m}\times(0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
by

F(\bm{x},r):=\frac{1}{r^{m}}\int_{B_{r}(\bm{x})}|f(\bm{y})-f(\bm{x})|\,d\bm{y}.

(537)

Then
\mathcal{L}=\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ \lim_{r\to 0^{+}}F(\bm{x},r)=0\}.
By definition of limits, the set
\mathcal{L}
might be rewritten as

\mathcal{L}=\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\bigcup_{\delta\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}}\bigcap_{r\in(0,\delta)}\left\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ F(\bm{x},r)\leq 2^{-k}\right\}.

(538)

Now, we show that the uncountable intersection over
r\in(0,\delta)
may be replaced with a countable intersection over only the rational points
r\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,\delta).
For this, it will suffice to show that, for each fixed
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},
the function
r\mapsto F(\bm{x},r)
is continuous over
r\in(0,\infty).
Clearly, the function
r\mapsto r^{-m}
is continuous. In addition, by the monotone convergence theorem, we obtain the continuity of the function

r\mapsto\int_{B_{r}(\bm{x})}|f(\bm{y})-f(\bm{x})|\,d\bm{y}.

(539)

Therefore,
r\mapsto F(\bm{x},r)
is continuous too. Thus, if
F(\bm{x},r)\leq 2^{-k}
for every
r\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,\delta),
the density of
\mathbb{Q}
in
\mathbb{R}
implies that
F(\bm{x},r)\leq 2^{-k}
for every
r\in(0,\delta).
Hence, we may write

\mathcal{L}=\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\bigcup_{\delta\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}}\bigcap_{r\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,\delta)}\left\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ F(\bm{x},r)\leq 2^{-k}\right\}.

(540)

Finally, we show that each set
\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ F(\bm{x},r)\leq 2^{-k}\}
is a Borel set (for fixed
r>0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
) by showing that
\bm{x}\mapsto F(\bm{x},r)
is a Borel function. Indeed, we may write
F(\bm{x},r)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}g(\bm{x},\bm{y})\,d\nu(\bm{y})
where
\nu
is the restriction of the Lebesgue measure of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
to the Borel subsets, and

g(\bm{x},\bm{y}):=\frac{1}{r^{m}}|f(\bm{y})-f(\bm{x})|\cdot 1_{B_{r}(\bm{x})}(\bm{y}).

(541)

Note that
1_{B_{r}(\bm{x})}(\bm{y})=1_{A}(\bm{x},\bm{y}),
where
A=\{(\bm{x},\bm{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{2m}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ \|\bm{x}-\bm{y}\|_{2}<r\}
is a Borel set. Thus,
g
is a Borel function. Therefore, by the Fubini-Tonelli theorem
[
54
, Section 2.5, Theorem 14]

\bm{x}\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}g(\bm{x},\bm{y})\,d\nu(\bm{y})
is a Borel function, and the proof is complete.
∎

I.1
Proof of Theorem
43

Denote
\delta=1/\sqrt{t},
and consider the
m
-RVs
\bm{Y}_{\delta}:=\bm{X}+\delta\bm{N}.
We have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{mmse}(\bm{X}\mid\sqrt{t}\bm{X}+\bm{N})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{mmse}(\bm{X}\mid\bm{X}+\delta\bm{N})=\left\|\bm{X}-\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{X}\mid\bm{Y}_{\delta}\right]\right\|_{2}^{2}=\left\|\bm{X}-\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}_{\delta}-\delta\bm{N}\mid\bm{Y}_{\delta}\right]\right\|_{2}^{2}

(542)

\displaystyle=\delta^{2}\left\|\bm{N}-\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{N}\mid\bm{Y}_{\delta}\right]\right\|_{2}^{2}=\delta^{2}\penalty\ \mathrm{mmse}\left(\bm{N}\mid\bm{Y}_{\delta}\right).

(543)

Therefore, the statement of the theorem is equivalent to

\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\mathrm{mmse}(\bm{N}\mid\bm{Y}_{\delta})=\mathrm{tr}\penalty\ \Sigma_{\bm{N}}.

(544)

To show that (
544
) holds, it suffices to show that

\liminf_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\penalty\ \mathrm{mmse}(\bm{N}\mid\bm{Y}_{\delta})\geq\mathrm{tr}\penalty\ \Sigma_{\bm{N}}.

(545)

Indeed, the constant estimator
\psi(\bm{y})=\mathbb{E}[\bm{N}]
attains an error of
\mathrm{tr}\penalty\ \Sigma_{\bm{N}}
when estimating
\bm{N}
given
\bm{Y}_{\delta},
hence

\limsup_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\penalty\ \mathrm{mmse}(\bm{N}\mid\bm{Y}_{\delta})\leq\mathrm{tr}\penalty\ \Sigma_{\bm{N}}.

(546)

Fix
\varepsilon>0.
By the square-integrability assumption on
\bm{N},
there is an
M\geq 0
such that

\left\|\bm{N}1_{(M,\infty)}(\|\bm{N}\|)\right\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon.

(547)

By the triangle inequality in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathcal{F}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{mmse}\left(\bm{N}\mid\bm{Y}_{\delta}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\bm{N}-\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{N}\mid\bm{Y}_{\delta}\right]\right\|_{2}

(548)

\displaystyle\geq\left\|\bm{N}-\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{N}1_{[0,M]}(\|\bm{N}\|)\mid\bm{Y}_{\delta}\right]\right\|_{2}-\left\|\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{N}1_{(M,\infty)}(\|\bm{N}\|)\mid\bm{Y}_{\delta}\right]\right\|_{2}.

(549)

Now, since
\left\|\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{N}1_{(M,\infty)}(\|\bm{N}\|)\mid\bm{Y}_{\delta}\right]\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\bm{N}1_{(M,\infty)}(\|\bm{N}\|)\right\|_{2},
we obtain from (
547
) that

\mathrm{mmse}\left(\bm{N}\mid\bm{Y}_{\delta}\right)^{1/2}\geq\left\|\bm{N}-\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{N}1_{[0,M]}(\|\bm{N}\|)\mid\bm{Y}_{\delta}\right]\right\|_{2}-\varepsilon.

(550)

Therefore, to obtain (
545
), it suffices to show that

\liminf_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\left\|\bm{N}-\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{N}1_{[0,M]}(\|\bm{N}\|)\mid\bm{Y}_{\delta}\right]\right\|_{2}\geq\sqrt{\mathrm{tr}\penalty\ \Sigma_{\bm{N}}}

(551)

since
\varepsilon
is arbitrary. Now, consider the function
\bm{f}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
given by

\bm{f}(\bm{x},\bm{z};\delta):=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{N}\penalty\ 1_{[0,M]}(\|\bm{N}\|)\mid\bm{Y}_{\delta}=\bm{x}+\delta\bm{z}\right],

(552)

and note that we have

\bm{f}(\bm{X},\bm{N};\delta)=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{N}\penalty\ 1_{[0,M]}(\|\bm{N}\|)\mid\bm{Y}_{\delta}\right].

(553)

We will show that

\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\bm{f}(\bm{X},\bm{N};\delta)=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{N}\penalty\ 1_{[0,M]}(\|\bm{N}\|)\right]

(554)

almost surely. The limit in (
554
) suffices to conclude the proof of the theorem, because it implies inequality (
551
) via Fatou’s lemma. Indeed, from (
554
) we obtain

\liminf_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\left\|\bm{N}-\bm{f}(\bm{X},\bm{N};\delta)\right\|_{2}\geq\left\|\bm{N}-\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{N}\penalty\ 1_{[0,M]}(\|\bm{N}\|)\right]\right\|_{2}\geq\left\|\bm{N}-\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{N}\right]\right\|_{2}=\sqrt{\mathrm{tr}\penalty\ \Sigma_{\bm{N}}},

(555)

where the penultimate step follows because
\mathbb{E}[\bm{N}]
is the best constant estimator of
\bm{N}
in the mean-square sense. Thus, the rest of the proof is devoted to showing that the limit in (
554
) holds.

Note that

p_{\bm{Y}_{\delta}}(\bm{y})=\frac{1}{\delta^{m}}\mathbb{E}\left[p_{\bm{N}}\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\left(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\right)\right)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[p_{\bm{X}}\left(\bm{y}-\delta\bm{N}\right)\right].

(556)

We have the conditional expectation formulas

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{X}\mid\bm{Y}_{\delta}=\bm{y}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{X}\penalty\ p_{\bm{N}}\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\left(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\right)\right)\right]\cdot\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}\left[p_{\bm{N}}\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\left(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\right)\right)\right]},

(557)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{N}\mid\bm{Y}_{\delta}=\bm{y}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{N}\penalty\ p_{\bm{X}}\left(\bm{y}-\delta\bm{N}\right)\right]\cdot\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}\left[p_{\bm{X}}\left(\bm{y}-\delta\bm{N}\right)\right]},

(558)

\displaystyle\bm{f}(\bm{x},\bm{z};\delta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{N}\penalty\ 1_{[0,M]}(\|\bm{N}\|)\penalty\ p_{\bm{X}}(\bm{x}+\delta\bm{z}-\delta\bm{N})\right]\cdot\frac{1}{p_{\bm{Y}_{\delta}}(\bm{x}+\delta\bm{z})}.

(559)

Define the function
\bm{g}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}

\displaystyle\bm{g}(\bm{x},\bm{z};\delta)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{N}\penalty\ 1_{[0,M]}(\|\bm{N}\|)\penalty\ p_{\bm{X}}(\bm{x}+\delta\bm{z}-\delta\bm{N})\right],

(560)

and note that

\bm{f}(\bm{x},\bm{z};\delta)=\bm{g}(\bm{x},\bm{z};\delta)\cdot\frac{1}{p_{\bm{Y}_{\delta}}(\bm{x}+\delta\bm{z})}.

(561)

Let
\mathcal{L}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be the Lebesgue set of
p_{\bm{X}},

\mathcal{S}:=\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ p_{\bm{X}}(\bm{x})>0\},
and
\mathcal{T}:=\mathcal{L}\cap\mathcal{S}.
We will show that the limit in (
554
) holds almost surely by showing that the following two limits hold for every
(\bm{x},\bm{z})\in\mathcal{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\bm{g}(\bm{x},\bm{z};\delta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{N}\penalty\ 1_{[0,M]}(\|\bm{N}\|)\right]p_{\bm{X}}(\bm{x}),

(562)

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}p_{\bm{Y}_{\delta}}(\bm{x}+\delta\bm{z})

\displaystyle=p_{\bm{X}}(\bm{x}).

(563)

We describe first how (
562
) and (
563
) imply (
554
). We have that
P_{\bm{X}}(\mathcal{S})=1
because
\mathcal{S}
is a Borel set and

P_{\bm{X}}(\mathcal{S}^{c})=\int_{\mathcal{S}^{c}}p_{\bm{X}}(\bm{x})\,d\bm{x}=\int_{\mathcal{S}^{c}}0\,d\bm{x}=0.

(564)

By the Lebesgue differentiation theorem, since
p_{\bm{X}}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m}),
the complement
\mathcal{L}^{c}
has zero Lebesgue measure. Further,
\mathcal{L}
is a Borel set by Lemma
54
, since
p_{\bm{X}}
is a Borel function. Thus, as
P_{\bm{X}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, we deduce that
P_{\bm{X}}(\mathcal{L}^{c})=0,
i.e.,
P_{\bm{X}}(\mathcal{L})=1.
Therefore,
P_{\bm{X}}(\mathcal{T})=1,
from which we obtain
P_{\bm{X},\bm{N}}(\mathcal{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{m})=P_{\bm{X}}(\mathcal{T})=1.
In other words, if (
562
) and (
563
) hold for every
(\bm{x},\bm{z})\in\mathcal{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
then (
554
) holds almost surely. We now focus on showing (
562
) and (
563
).

Fix
\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},
and denote
\bm{f}=(f_{1},\cdots,f_{m})^{T}
and
\bm{g}=(g_{1},\cdots,g_{m})^{T}.
We apply Theorem
53
. The following convolution relations hold for every
j\in\{1,\cdots,\ell\}

\displaystyle g_{j}(\bm{x},\bm{z};\delta)

\displaystyle=(p_{\bm{X}}\ast G_{\delta,j})(\bm{x})

(565)

\displaystyle p_{\bm{Y}_{\delta}}(\bm{x}+\delta\bm{z})

\displaystyle=(p_{\bm{X}}\ast K_{\delta})(\bm{x}),

(566)

where the functions
G_{\delta,j}
and
K_{\delta}
are defined by

\displaystyle G_{\delta,j}(\bm{y})

\displaystyle:=(\delta^{-1}y_{j}+z_{j})1_{[0,M]}\left(\left\|\delta^{-1}\bm{y}+\bm{z}\right\|\right)p_{\bm{N}}\left(\delta^{-1}\bm{y}+\bm{z}\right)\delta^{-m}

(567)

\displaystyle K_{\delta}(\bm{y})

\displaystyle:=\delta^{-m}p_{\bm{N}}\left(\delta^{-1}\bm{y}+\bm{z}\right).

(568)

Indeed, denoting
q_{j}(\bm{u})=u_{j}1_{[0,M]}\left(\left\|\bm{u}\right\|\right),
and using the substitution
\bm{y}=\delta(\bm{u}-\bm{z}),
we may compute

\displaystyle(p_{\bm{X}}\ast G_{\delta,j})(\bm{x})

\displaystyle\hskip-1.42262pt=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}p_{\bm{X}}(\bm{x}-\bm{y})q_{j}(\delta^{-1}\bm{y}+\bm{z})p_{\bm{N}}\left(\delta^{-1}\bm{y}+\bm{z}\right)\delta^{-m}d\bm{y}

(569)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}p_{\bm{X}}(x-\delta(\bm{u}-\bm{z}))q_{j}(\bm{u})p_{\bm{N}}(\bm{u})\,d\bm{w}=\mathbb{E}\left[p_{\bm{X}}(\bm{x}-\delta(\bm{N}-\bm{z}))q_{j}(\bm{N})\right]=g_{j}(\bm{x},\bm{z};\delta).

(570)

Also, noting that

K_{\delta}(\bm{y})=\delta^{-m}p_{\bm{N}}(\delta^{-1}\bm{y}+\bm{z})=p_{\delta(\bm{N}-\bm{z})}(\bm{y}),

(571)

and by independence of
\bm{X}
and
\delta(\bm{N}-\bm{z}),
we obtain the convolution

\displaystyle(p_{\bm{X}}\ast K_{\delta})(\bm{x})=(p_{\bm{X}}\ast p_{\delta(\bm{N}-\bm{z})})(\bm{x})=p_{\bm{X}+\delta(\bm{N}-\bm{z})}(\bm{x})=p_{\bm{Y}_{\delta}}(\bm{x}+\delta\bm{z}).

(572)

Therefore, equations (
565
) and (
566
) hold. Fix
j\in\{1,\cdots,m\}.
We show next that
\{G_{\delta,j}\}_{\delta>0}
and
\{K_{\delta}\}_{\delta>0}
are approximations to the identity. For condition
(i)
of Definition
11
, we note that the substitution
\bm{y}=\delta(\bm{u}-\bm{z})
implies that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}G_{\delta,j}(\bm{y})\,d\bm{y}=\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}1_{[0,M]}(\|\bm{N}\|)\right],

(573)

which is a constant independent of
\delta.
Also, since
K_{\delta}
is the PDF of
\delta(\bm{N}-\bm{z})
(see (
571
)), we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}K_{\delta}(\bm{y})\,d\bm{y}=1.

(574)

Therefore, condition
(i)
in Definition
11
is satisfied by both the
G_{\delta,j}
and the
K_{\delta}.
We now show that the bounds in conditions
(ii)
and
(iii)
of Definition
11
hold with the constant
B
chosen as
B:=(1+\|\bm{z}\|)^{m+1}C
where
C>0
is any constant such that

p_{\bm{N}}(\bm{u})\leq\frac{C}{1+\|\bm{u}\|^{m+2}}

(575)

holds for every
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.
Note that such a constant
C
exists by assumption (
308
) on the decay of
p_{\bm{N}}.

Since
v^{\beta}\leq 1+v^{m+2}
for every
v\geq 0
and
\beta\in[0,m+2],
inequality (
575
) implies the uniform bounds

\|\bm{u}\|^{\beta}p_{\bm{N}}(\bm{u})\leq C

(576)

for every
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\beta\in[0,m+2].
Denote

S_{\beta}:=\sup_{\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}}\|\bm{u}\|^{\beta}p_{\bm{N}}(\bm{u}),

(577)

so
S_{\beta}\leq C\leq B
for every
0\leq\beta\leq m+2.
For condition
(ii)
, we have that for every
\delta>0
and
\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},

\delta^{m}|G_{\delta,j}(\bm{y})|\leq\left|\delta^{-1}y_{j}+z_{j}\right|p_{\bm{N}}(\delta^{-1}\bm{y}+\bm{z})\leq\left\|\delta^{-1}\bm{y}+\bm{z}\right\|p_{\bm{N}}(\delta^{-1}\bm{y}+\bm{z})\leq S_{1}\leq C\leq B

(578)

and

\delta^{m}K_{\delta}(\bm{y})=p_{\bm{N}}(\delta^{-1}\bm{y}+\bm{z})\leq S_{0}\leq C\leq B.

(579)

Therefore, condition
(ii)
of Definition
11
is satisfied by both the
G_{\delta,j}
and the
K_{\delta}.
Finally, for condition
(iii)
, noting that

\left\|\delta^{-1}\bm{y}\right\|\leq\left\|\delta^{-1}\bm{y}+\bm{z}\right\|+\left\|\bm{z}\right\|,

(580)

we have the inequalities

\displaystyle\frac{\|\bm{y}\|^{m+1}}{\delta}|G_{\delta}(\bm{y})|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\delta^{-1}\bm{y}\right\|^{m+1}\left|\delta^{-1}y_{j}+z_{j}\right|p_{\bm{N}}(\delta^{-1}\bm{y}+\bm{z})

(581)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\left\|\delta^{-1}\bm{y}+\bm{z}\right\|+\left\|\bm{z}\right\|\right)^{m+1}\left\|\delta^{-1}\bm{y}+\bm{z}\right\|p_{\bm{N}}(\delta^{-1}\bm{y}+\bm{z})

(582)

\displaystyle\leq\left(S_{m+2}^{1/(m+1)}+\|\bm{z}\|S_{1}^{1/(m+1)}\right)^{m+1}\leq(1+\|\bm{z}\|)^{m+1}C=B

(583)

and

\displaystyle\frac{\|\bm{y}\|^{m+1}}{\delta}K_{\delta}(\bm{y})=\left\|\delta^{-1}\bm{y}\right\|^{m+1}p_{\bm{N}}(\delta^{-1}\bm{y}+\bm{z})\leq\left(S_{m+1}^{1/(m+1)}+\|\bm{z}\|S_{0}^{1/(m+1)}\right)^{m+1}\leq(1+\|\bm{z}\|)^{m+1}C=B

(584)

for every
\delta>0
and
\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.
Therefore, condition
(iii)
in Definition
11
is satisfied by both the
G_{\delta,j}
and the
K_{\delta}.
In other words, each of
\{G_{\delta,j}\}_{\delta>0}
and
\{K_{\delta}\}_{\delta>0}
is an approximation to the identity.

Therefore, by Theorem
53
, for each
j\in\{1,\cdots,m\}
and
(\bm{x},\bm{z})\in\mathcal{L}\times\mathbb{R}^{m},
we have that

\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}g_{j}(\bm{x},\bm{z};\delta)=\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}1_{[0,M]}(\|\bm{N}\|)\right]p_{\bm{X}}(\bm{x})

(585)

and

\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}p_{\bm{Y}_{\delta}}(\bm{x}+\delta\bm{z})=p_{\bm{X}}(\bm{x}).

(586)

Hence, by (
561
), we obtain that for every
(\bm{x},\bm{z})\in\mathcal{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}

\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\bm{f}(\bm{X},\bm{N};\delta)=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{N}\penalty\ 1_{[0,M]}(\|\bm{N}\|)\right],

(587)

which is the desired limit (
554
), completing the proof.

Appendix J
Proofs of Subsection
11.2

J.1
Proof of Theorem
48
: Consistency of the Differential Entropy Estimator

We use the formula for
h_{n}
given in Lemma
46
,

h_{n}(X)=\frac{1}{2}\log\left(2\pi e\left(\frac{\det\bm{M}_{X,n}}{\det\bm{M}_{N,n}}\right)^{1/d_{n}}\right)+\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho_{X,n}(t)\,dt,

(588)

where
d_{n}=\binom{n+1}{2}
and
N\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1).
We may assume that
N
is independent of
X
and the
X_{j}.
For each
m\in\mathbb{N},
let
\mathcal{S}_{m}:=\{X_{j}\}_{j\in[m]},
and consider the sequence
\{U_{m}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{S}_{m})\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}.
For each
m\in\mathbb{N},
let
\mathfrak{E}_{m}
be the event that
X_{0},\cdots,X_{m}
are distinct, and let
\mathfrak{E}
be the event that the
X_{j},
for
j\in\mathbb{N},
are all distinct. Whenever
m\geq n
and
\mathfrak{E}_{m}
occurs, we have by Definition
9
of
\widehat{h}_{n}
and formula (
588
) for
h_{n}
the following estimate

\widehat{h}_{n}\left(\mathcal{S}_{m}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\log\left(2\pi e\left(\frac{\det\bm{M}_{U_{m},n}}{\det\bm{M}_{N,n}}\right)^{1/d_{n}}\right)+\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho_{U_{m},n}(t)\,dt.

(589)

Since
X
is continuous, we have that
P(\mathfrak{E}_{m})=1
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}.
Further,
\mathfrak{E}_{0}\supset\mathfrak{E}_{1}\supset\cdots
and
\mathfrak{E}=\bigcap_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{E}_{m},
hence
P(\mathfrak{E})=1.
Therefore, for the purpose of proving the almost-sure limit
\widehat{h}_{n}\left(\mathcal{S}_{m}\right)\to h_{n}(X),
we may assume that
\mathfrak{E}
occurs. We first treat convergence of the integral part. We show that the integral part is a continuous function of the moments, then the continuous mapping theorem yields that

\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho_{U_{m},n}(t)\,dt\to\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho_{X,n}(t)\,dt

(590)

almost surely as
m\to\infty
because sample moments converge almost surely to the moments. A similar method is then applied to the convergence of the
\log\det\bm{M}_{X,n}
part.

We fix
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},
and assume
m\geq n
throughout the proof. We use the following notation. The
2n
-RV
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(m)}
consists of the first
2n
moments of
U_{m}

\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(m)}:=\left(\frac{\sum_{j=0}^{m}X_{j}}{m+1},\cdots,\frac{\sum_{j=0}^{m}X_{j}^{2n}}{m+1}\right)^{T}.

(591)

Let
\mu^{(m)}_{k}
be the
k
-th coordinate of
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(m)},
so
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(m)}=\left(\mu_{1}^{(m)},\cdots,\mu_{2n}^{(m)}\right)^{T}.
We write
\mathcal{X}_{k}:=\mathbb{E}\left[X^{k}\right]
for
k\in\mathbb{N},
and consider the constant vector

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}:=\left(\mathcal{X}_{k}\right)_{1\leq k\leq 2n}.

(592)

By the strong law of large numbers, we have the almost-sure convergence
\mu_{k}^{(m)}\to\mathcal{X}_{k}
for each
1\leq k\leq 2n.
Then,
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(m)}\to\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}
almost surely as
m\to\infty.
We show next that the function
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}\mapsto\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho_{X,n}(t)\,dt
is continuous.

By definition of
\rho_{X,n}
(see (
342
)), there are polynomials
A_{1},\cdots,A_{d_{n}-2}
and
B_{1},\cdots,B_{d_{n}}
in
2n
variables such that

\rho_{X,n}(t)=\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}-2}A_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})\penalty\ t^{j}}{c_{n}+\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}}B_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})\penalty\ t^{j}}

(593)

where
c_{n}:=\prod_{k=1}^{n}k!
(we are subsuming the
1/2
factor in (
342
) in the numerator, so we have the equality
\delta_{X,n}(t)=c_{n}+\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}}B_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})t^{j}
). Being polynomials, each of the
A_{j}
and the
B_{\ell}
is continuous over
\mathbb{R}^{2n}.
Then, by the continuous mapping theorem, we have the almost-sure convergences

A_{j}\left({\bm{\mu}}^{(m)}\right)\to A_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})\quad\text{and}\quad B_{\ell}\left({\bm{\mu}}^{(m)}\right)\to B_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})

(594)

as
m\to\infty
for each
1\leq j\leq d_{n}-2
and
1\leq\ell\leq d_{n}.
Denote

\displaystyle\bm{A}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})

\displaystyle:=\left(A_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})\right)_{1\leq j\leq d_{n}-2},

(595)

\displaystyle\bm{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})

\displaystyle:=\left(B_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})\right)_{1\leq j\leq d_{n}}.

(596)

We show next that the there is an open set
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
containing the point
\bm{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})
such that the mapping
f:\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}-2}\times\mathcal{O}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

f(p_{1},\cdots,p_{d_{n}-2},q_{1},\cdots,q_{d_{n}}):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}-2}p_{j}t^{j}}{c_{n}+\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}}q_{j}t^{j}}\,dt

(597)

is continuous at the point
(\bm{A}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}),\,\bm{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})).
To this end, we shall show first that the mapping in (
597
) is well-defined on an open neighborhood of
(\bm{A}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}),\,\bm{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})).
In other words, the denominator of the integrand
t\mapsto c_{n}+\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}}q_{j}t^{j}
cannot have a root
t\in[0,\infty)
for any
\bm{q}\in\mathcal{O},
and the rational function integrand has to be integrable. For integrability, we will restrict the set
\mathcal{O}
to contain only points having
q_{d_{n}}>0,
so showing that the integrand’s denominator is strictly positive over
t\in[0,\infty)
will be enough to deduce integrability in (
597
).

We consider the subset
\mathcal{G}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
defined by

\mathcal{G}:=\left\{\bm{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ g_{d_{n}}>0,\text{ and }\sum_{\ell=1}^{d_{n}}g_{j}t^{j}>-c_{n}\;\text{ for every }t\geq 0\right\}

(598)

where in this definition and the subsequent argument we set
{\bm{g}}=\left(g_{1},\cdots,g_{d_{n}}\right)^{T}.
Note that
\bm{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})\in\mathcal{G}.
Indeed, since
X
is continuous,
B_{d_{n}}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})=\det\bm{M}_{X,n}>0
; similarly, for every
t\in[0,\infty),
continuity of
\sqrt{t}X+N
implies that
\det\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N}>0
(recall that
c_{n}+\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}}B_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})t^{j}=\det\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N}
). We show that
\mathcal{G}
is an open set. Fix
\bm{g}\in\mathcal{G}
and
\varepsilon_{1}\in\left(0,g_{d_{n}}\right).
We have that the polynomial
\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}}(g_{j}-\varepsilon_{1})t^{j}
is eventually increasing and approaches infinity as
t\to\infty.
Let
t_{0}>1
be such that for every
t>t_{0}
we have

\sum_{\ell=1}^{d_{n}}(g_{j}-\varepsilon_{1})t^{j}>-c_{n}.

(599)

Being continuous, the polynomial
\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}}g_{j}t^{j}
attains its minimum over the compact set
[0,t_{0}].
Let
s
denote this minimum, and note that
s>-c_{n}.
Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
be defined by

\varepsilon:=\frac{1}{2}\min\left(\varepsilon_{1},\frac{(s+c_{n})(t_{0}-1)}{t_{0}(t_{0}^{d_{n}}-1)}\right).

(600)

As
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{1},
inequality (
599
) yields that for every
t>t_{0}

\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}}(g_{j}-\varepsilon)t^{j}>-c_{n}.

(601)

In addition, for any
t\in[0,t_{0}],

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}}(g_{j}-\varepsilon)t^{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}}g_{j}t^{j}-\varepsilon\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}}t^{j}\geq s-\varepsilon\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}}t_{0}^{j}>s-\frac{(s+c_{n})(t_{0}-1)}{t_{0}(t_{0}^{d_{n}}-1)}\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}}t_{0}^{j}=s-(s+c_{n})=-c_{n}.

(602)

Thus, combining (
601
) and (
602
) we obtain

\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}}(g_{j}-\varepsilon)t^{j}>-c_{n}

(603)

for every
t\in[0,\infty).
Hence, for any
(\delta_{j})_{1\leq j\leq d_{n}}=:{\bm{\delta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
such that
\|{\bm{\delta}}\|_{2}<\varepsilon,
we have that for all
t\in[0,\infty)

\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}}(g_{j}-\delta_{j})t^{j}\geq\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}}(g_{j}-\|{\bm{\delta}}\|_{2})t^{j}\geq\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}}(g_{j}-\varepsilon)t^{j}>-c_{n}.

(604)

In other words, the open ball
\{\bm{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ \|\bm{q}-\bm{g}\|<\varepsilon\}
lies within
\mathcal{G}.
This completes the proof that
\mathcal{G}
is open. Then, the function
f
given by (
597
) is well-defined on the open set
\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}-2}\times\mathcal{G}.
We will replace
\mathcal{G}
with an open box
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathcal{G}
to simplify the notation for the proof of continuity of
f.

By openness of
\mathcal{G},
there is an
\eta_{1}\in(0,B_{d_{n}}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}))
such that the open box

\mathcal{O}_{1}:=\prod_{j=1}^{d_{n}}\left(B_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-\eta_{1},B_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})+\eta_{1}\right)\subset\mathcal{G}

(605)

contains
\bm{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}).
Since
\mathcal{O}_{1}\subset\mathcal{G},
we have by the definition of
\mathcal{G}
in (
598
) that for any
\bm{g}\in\mathcal{O}_{1}
the lower bound

c_{n}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{d_{n}}g_{\ell}t^{\ell}>0

(606)

holds for every
t\geq 0.
In particular, with
\eta:=\eta_{1}/2,
the set

\mathcal{O}:=\prod_{j=1}^{d_{n}}\left(B_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-\eta,B_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})+\eta\right)\subset\mathcal{O}_{1}\subset\mathcal{G}

(607)

is an open set containing
\bm{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}),
and the point
(B_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-\eta)_{1\leq j\leq 2n}
lies inside
\mathcal{G}.
Then, the function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}-2}\times\mathcal{O}\to\mathbb{R}
given by (
597
) is well-defined, and for any
\bm{g}\in\mathcal{O}
we have the lower bound (over
t\in[0,\infty)
)

c_{n}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{d_{n}}g_{\ell}t^{\ell}\geq c_{n}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{d_{n}}(B_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-\eta)t^{\ell}>0.

(608)

From (
608
), Lebesgue’s dominated convergence shows continuity of
f
at
(\bm{A}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}),\bm{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})),
as follows.

Let
{\bm{w}}:=({\bm{u}},{\bm{v}})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}-2}\times\mathcal{O}
be such that
\|{\bm{w}}\|_{2}<\eta.
The integrand in
f
at
(\bm{A}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}),\bm{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}))-({\bm{u}},{\bm{v}})
may be bounded as

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}-2}(A_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-u_{j})t^{j}}{c_{n}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{d_{n}}(B_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-v_{\ell})t^{\ell}}\right|=\frac{\left|\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}-2}(A_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-u_{j})t^{j}\right|}{c_{n}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{d_{n}}(B_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-v_{\ell})t^{\ell}}\leq\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}-2}(|A_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})|+\eta)t^{j}}{c_{n}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{d_{n}}(B_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-\eta)t^{\ell}}.

(609)

The bound in (
609
) is uniform in
\bm{w},
and the upper bound is integrable over
[0,\infty)
as the denominator’s degree exceeds that of the numerator by at least
2
and the denominator is strictly positive by (
608
). Hence, by Lebesgue’s dominated convergence

\lim_{\|{\bm{w}}\|\to 0}f\left((\bm{A}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}),\bm{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}))-{\bm{w}}\right)=f\left(\bm{A}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}),\bm{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})\right),

(610)

i.e.,
f
is continuous at
(\bm{A}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}),\bm{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})),
as desired. Denote

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{(m)}

\displaystyle:=\left(A_{j}({\bm{\mu}}^{(m)})\right)_{1\leq j\leq d_{n}-2},

(611)

\displaystyle\bm{B}^{(m)}

\displaystyle:=\left(B_{\ell}({\bm{\mu}}^{(m)})\right)_{1\leq\ell\leq d_{n}}.

(612)

We have the formulas

f(\bm{A}^{(m)},\bm{B}^{(m)})=\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho_{U_{m},n}(t)\,dt

(613)

and

f(\bm{A}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}),\bm{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}))=\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho_{X,n}(t)\,dt.

(614)

Since
(\bm{A}^{(m)},\bm{B}^{(m)})\to(\bm{A}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}),\bm{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}))
almost surely, continuity of
f
at
(\bm{A}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}),\bm{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}))
implies by the continuous mapping theorem that

f(\bm{A}^{(m)},\bm{B}^{(m)})\to f(\bm{A}({\bm{\nu}}),\bm{B}({\bm{\nu}}))

(615)

almost surely as
m\to\infty,
i.e., (
590
) holds.

Now, for the convergence of the logarithmic part, recall that we have the almost sure convergence

\det\bm{M}_{U_{m},n}=B_{d_{n}}({\bm{\mu}}^{(m)})\to B_{d_{n}}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})=\det\bm{M}_{X,n}

(616)

as
m\to\infty.
As the mapping
\mathbb{R}_{>0}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by
q\mapsto\log q
is continuous, the continuous mapping theorem yields that

\log\det\bm{M}_{U_{m},n}\to\log\det\bm{M}_{X,n}

(617)

almost surely as
m\to\infty.
Combining (
615
) and (
617
), we obtain that

\widehat{h}_{n}\left(\mathcal{S}_{m}\right)\to h_{n}(X)

(618)

almost surely as
m\to\infty.
Finally, (
347
) follows from (
618
) by Theorem
32
.

J.2
Proof of Corollary
49
: Consistency of the Mutual Information Estimator

Denote
\mathcal{S}_{m}=\{(X_{j},Y_{j})\}_{j\in[m]},
and consider the empirical measure

\widehat{P}_{m}(x):=\sum_{j\in[m]}\frac{\delta_{x}(X_{j})}{m+1}.

(619)

Let
\mathfrak{D}_{m}
be the event that for each
x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)
there is a subset of indices
J_{x}\subset[m]
of size at least
n+1
such that:
i)

X_{j}=x
for each
j\in J_{x},
and
ii)
the
Y_{j},
for
j\in J_{x},
are distinct. If
\mathfrak{D}_{m}
occurs, then we may write

\widehat{I}_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{m})=\widehat{h}_{n}(\mathcal{A}_{m})-\sum_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)}\widehat{P}_{m}(x)\penalty\ \widehat{h}_{n}(\mathcal{B}_{m,x}),

(620)

where
\mathcal{A}_{m}:=\{Y_{j}\}_{j\in[m]}
and
\mathcal{B}_{m,x}:=\{Y_{j}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ j\in[m],X_{j}=x\}.
By the assumption of continuity of
Y,
it holds with probability
1
that the
Y_{j},
for
j\in\mathbb{N},
are all distinct. In addition, we have that
P_{X}(x)>0
for each
x\in\mathrm{supp}(X).
Therefore,
P(\mathfrak{D}_{m})\to 1
as
m\to\infty.
Note that
\mathfrak{D}_{0}\subset\mathfrak{D}_{1}\subset\cdots.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be the event that
\lim_{m\to\infty}\widehat{h}_{n}(\mathcal{A}_{m})=h_{n}(Y)
and, for each
x\in\mathrm{supp}(X),

\lim_{m\to\infty}\widehat{h}_{n}(\mathcal{B}_{m,x})=h_{n}(Y^{(x)}).
By Theorem
48
and finiteness of
\mathrm{supp}(X),
for each integer
m^{\prime}\geq(n+1)|\mathrm{supp}(X)|,
we have that
P(\mathfrak{C}\mid\mathfrak{D}_{m^{\prime}})=1.
Let
\mathfrak{F}
be the event that the empirical measure
\widehat{P}_{m}
converges to
P_{X},
i.e., that for each
x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)
the limit
\widehat{P}_{m}(x)\to P_{X}(x)
holds as
m\to\infty.
By the strong law of large numbers,
P(\mathfrak{F})=1.
Therefore,

P\left(\lim_{m\to\infty}\widehat{I}_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{m})=I(X;Y)\right)\geq P(\mathfrak{C}\cap\mathfrak{F}\cap\mathfrak{D}_{m^{\prime}})\geq P(\mathfrak{F})+P(\mathfrak{C}\cap\mathfrak{D}_{m^{\prime}})-1=P(\mathfrak{D}_{m^{\prime}}).

(621)

Taking
m^{\prime}\to\infty,
we deduce that
\widehat{I}_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{m})\to I(X;Y)
almost surely.

Appendix K
Proofs of Subsection
11.3
: Sample Complexity

K.1
Proof of Proposition
50
: Differential Entropy

Suppose
\mathrm{supp}(X)\subset[p,q]\subset(0,\infty),
and write
\mathcal{S}=\{X_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
; note that we may assume, without loss of generality, that
X
is strictly positive because
h_{n}
is shift-invariant. We use the same notation in Appendix
J
. In particular,
\mathcal{X}_{k}=\mathbb{E}[X^{k}],
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}=(\mathcal{X}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{X}_{2n})^{T}.
Let
U\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{S}).
Let
\mathfrak{E}_{m}
be the event that
X_{1},\cdots,X_{m}
are distinct. From equations (
588
) and (
589
), if
m>n
and
\mathfrak{E}_{m}
holds, then we have that

\widehat{h}_{n}(\mathcal{S})-h_{n}(X)=\frac{1}{2d_{n}}\log\frac{\det\bm{M}_{U,n}}{\det\bm{M}_{X,n}}+\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho_{U,n}(t)\,-\rho_{X,n}(t)\,dt.

(622)

By the assumption of continuity of
X,
we have that
P(\mathfrak{E}_{m})=1
for every
m.
Therefore, for the purpose of proving a sample complexity bound, we may assume that
m>n
and that
\mathfrak{E}_{m}
occurs.

We will consider the determinant part and the integral part in (
622
) separately, but the proof technique will be the same. Let
A_{j}
and
B_{\ell}
be the polynomials as defined by equation (
593
) in Appendix
J
, so

\rho_{X,n}(t)=\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}-2}A_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})\penalty\ t^{j}}{c_{n}+\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}}B_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})\penalty\ t^{j}}

(623)

where
c_{n}:=\prod_{k=1}^{n}j!.
We split each of the polynomials
A_{j}
and
B_{\ell}
into a positive part and a negative part. More precisely, we collect the terms in
A_{j}
that have positive coefficients into a polynomial
A_{j}^{(+)},
and the terms in
A_{j}
with negative coefficients into a polynomial
-A_{j}^{(-)}
(so
A_{j}^{(-)}
has positive coefficients, and
A_{j}=A_{j}^{(+)}-A_{j}^{(-)}
). Define
B_{\ell}^{(+)}
and
B_{\ell}^{(-)}
from
B_{\ell}
similarly. By positivity of
X,
each moment
\mathcal{X}_{k}
is (strictly) positive. Then, we may write

\rho_{X,n}(t)=\frac{f_{X}(t)-g_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}

(624)

with the polynomials in
t

\displaystyle f_{X}(t)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}-2}A_{j}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})t^{j}

(625)

\displaystyle g_{X}(t)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}-2}A_{j}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})t^{j}

(626)

\displaystyle u_{X}(t)

\displaystyle:=c_{n}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{d_{n}}B_{\ell}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})t^{\ell}

(627)

\displaystyle v_{X}(t)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\ell=1}^{d_{n}}B_{\ell}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})t^{\ell},

(628)

having all non-negative coefficients. We note that we have suppressed the dependence on
n
in the notation used for these polynomials for readability. For
q\in\{f,g,u,v\},
let
q_{U}
be the random variable whose value is what is obtained via
q_{X}
when the moments of
X
are replaced with the sample moments obtained from the samples
\mathcal{S},
e.g.,

f_{U}(t):=\sum_{j=1}^{d_{n}-2}A_{j}^{(+)}\left(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}}{m},\cdots,\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}^{2n}}{m}\right)t^{j}.

(629)

Note that
u_{U}(t)-v_{U}(t)=\det\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}U+N,n}>0,
where
N\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
is independent of
X,X_{1},\cdots,X_{m}.
Then the function

\rho_{U,n}(t)=\frac{f_{U}(t)-g_{U}(t)}{u_{U}(t)-v_{U}(t)}

(630)

is well-defined over
t\in[0,\infty).
By the homogeneity properties proved in Theorem
18
, we know that the total degree of
A_{j}
is at most
2j+2,
and the total degree of
B_{\ell}
is at most
2\ell.
Therefore, for any
\eta\in(0,1)
and
(\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{2n})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2n},
we have the inequalities

\displaystyle(1-\eta)^{2j+2}A_{j}^{(\pm)}(\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{2n})

\displaystyle\leq A_{j}^{(\pm)}((1-\eta)\xi_{1},\cdots,(1-\eta)\xi_{2n})

(631)

\displaystyle A_{j}^{(\pm)}((1+\eta)\xi_{1},\cdots,(1+\eta)\xi_{2n})

\displaystyle\leq(1+\eta)^{2j+2}A_{j}^{(\pm)}(\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{2n})

(632)

\displaystyle(1-\eta)^{2\ell}B_{\ell}^{(\pm)}(\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{2n})

\displaystyle\leq B_{\ell}^{(\pm)}((1-\eta)\xi_{1},\cdots,(1-\eta)\xi_{2n})

(633)

\displaystyle B_{\ell}^{(\pm)}((1+\eta)\xi_{1},\cdots,(1+\eta)\xi_{2n})

\displaystyle\leq(1+\eta)^{2\ell}B_{\ell}^{(\pm)}(\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{2n})

(634)

for every
1\leq j\leq d_{n}-2
and
1\leq\ell\leq d_{n}.

For each
\eta\in(0,1),
we denote the event

\mathfrak{A}_{n,\eta}(\mathcal{S}):=\left\{1-\eta\leq\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}^{k}}{m\mathcal{X}_{k}}\leq 1+\eta\text{ for every }k\in[2n]\right\},

(635)

Hoeffding’s inequality yields that, for any
z>0
and
1\leq k\leq 2n,

P\left(\left|\mathcal{X}_{k}-\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}^{k}\right|\geq z\right)\leq 2e^{-2mz^{2}/\left(q^{k}-p^{k}\right)^{2}}.

(636)

Setting
z=\eta\mathcal{X}_{k}\geq\eta p^{k}>0
for
\eta\in(0,1)
yields that

P\left((1-\eta)\mathcal{X}_{k}<\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}^{k}<(1+\eta)\mathcal{X}_{k}\right)\geq 1-2e^{-2m\eta^{2}/\left((q/p)^{k}-1\right)^{2}}.

(637)

Therefore, the union bound yields that

P\left(\mathfrak{A}_{n,\eta}(\mathcal{S})\right)\geq 1-4ne^{-2m\eta^{2}/\left((q/p)^{2n}-1\right)^{2}}.

(638)

If
\mathfrak{A}_{n,\eta}(\mathcal{S})
occurs, we show a bound on the estimation error that is linear in
\eta

\widehat{h}_{n}(\mathcal{S})-h_{n}(X)=O_{X,n}(\eta),

(639)

independent of the number of samples
m,
for all small enough
\eta.
Then, we choose
\eta
to be linear in the error
\varepsilon
to conclude the proof.

We may bound
\rho_{U,n}(t)
(see (
630
)) via the bounds in (
631
)–(
634
) under the assumption that
\mathfrak{A}_{n,\eta}(\mathcal{S})
occurs. If
(1-\eta)\mathcal{X}_{k}\leq\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}^{m}\leq(1+\eta)\mathcal{X}_{k}
holds for every
1\leq k\leq 2n,
then by (
631
)–(
634
) we have that for every
t\geq 0
and
\eta\in(0,1)

\frac{(1-\eta)^{2}f_{X}((1-\eta)^{2}t)-(1+\eta)^{2}g_{X}((1+\eta)^{2}t)}{u_{X}((1+\eta)^{2}t)-v_{X}((1-\eta)^{2}t)}\leq\frac{f_{U}(t)-g_{U}(t)}{u_{U}(t)-v_{U}(t)}=\rho_{U,n}(t).

(640)

For an analogous upper bound, we first verify the positivity

u_{X}((1-\eta)^{2}t)-v_{X}((1+\eta)^{2}t)>0

(641)

for every small enough
\eta.
Let

\mu_{X}:=\sup_{t\in[0,\infty)}\frac{v_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)}.

(642)

We show that
\mu_{X}<1.
We have the limit

\xi_{X}:=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{v_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)}=\frac{B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}.

(643)

Recall that
B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})=B_{d_{n}}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})=\det\bm{M}_{X,n}>0
and both
B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})
and
B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})
are non-negative, hence
B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})>0.
Then,
\xi_{X}<1.
Thus, there is a
t_{0}\geq 0
such that
v_{X}(t)/u_{X}(t)<(1+\xi_{X})/2<1
whenever
t>t_{0}.
Further, by the extreme value theorem, there is a
t_{1}\in[0,t_{0}]
such that
v_{X}(t)/u_{X}(t)\leq v_{X}(t_{1})/u_{X}(t_{1})<1
for every
t\in[0,t_{0}].
Therefore,
\mu_{X}\leq\max((1+\xi_{X})/2,v_{X}(t_{1})/u_{X}(t_{1}))<1,
as desired. Note that if
\mu_{X}=0
then
v_{X}\equiv 0
identically, in which case (
641
) trivially holds by positivity of
u_{X}.
So, for the purpose of showing (
641
), it suffices to consider the case
\mu_{X}\in(0,1).
Denote

\nu:=\left(\frac{1+\eta}{1-\eta}\right)^{2}.

(644)

Now, since
v_{X}
is a polynomial of degree at most
d_{n},
we have that
v_{X}(\alpha\tau)\leq\alpha^{d_{n}}v_{X}(\tau)
for every
\alpha\geq 1
and
\tau\geq 0.
Therefore, for every
1\leq\nu<\mu_{X}^{-1/d_{n}}
and
t\geq 0,
we have that

\frac{v_{X}((1+\eta)^{2}t)}{u_{X}((1-\eta)^{2}t)}\leq\left(\frac{1+\eta}{1-\eta}\right)^{2d_{n}}\cdot\frac{v_{X}((1-\eta)^{2}t)}{u_{X}((1-\eta)^{2}t)}\leq\nu^{d_{n}}\mu_{X}<1,

(645)

i.e., inequality (
641
) holds. Therefore, for every
1\leq\nu<\mu_{X}^{-1/d_{n}}
(if
\mu_{X}=0,
we allow
1\leq\nu<\infty
), inequalities (
631
)–(
634
) imply the bound

\rho_{U,n}(t)=\frac{f_{U}(t)-g_{U}(t)}{u_{U}(t)-v_{U}(t)}\leq\frac{(1+\eta)^{2}f_{X}((1+\eta)^{2}t)-(1-\eta)^{2}g_{X}((1-\eta)^{2}t)}{u_{X}((1-\eta)^{2}t)-v_{X}((1+\eta)^{2}t)}.

(646)

Combining (
640
) and (
646
), then integrating with respect to
t
over
[0,\infty)
and performing a change of variables from
t
to
(1-\eta)^{2}t,
we obtain the bounds

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{f_{X}(t)-\nu g_{X}(\nu t)}{u_{X}(\nu t)-v_{X}(t)}\,dt\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho_{U,n}(t)\,dt\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\nu f_{X}(\nu t)-g_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(\nu t)}\,dt.

(647)

Next, we further develop these bounds. For any
s\in(0,1),
denote

\nu_{X,n,s}:=\left(\frac{1-s\mu_{X}}{1-s}\right)^{1/d_{n}}.

(648)

Consider the functions

\displaystyle\varphi_{X}(t;\nu)

\displaystyle:=\frac{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(\nu t)},

(649)

\displaystyle\psi_{X}(t;\nu)

\displaystyle:=\frac{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(\nu t)-v_{X}(t)}.

(650)

We show in Appendix
K.2
that, for any
s\in(0,(1-\mu_{X})/(1+\mu_{X}))
and
1\leq\nu\leq\nu_{X,n,s},
the uniform bounds

1-s\leq\psi_{X}(t;\nu)\leq 1\leq\varphi_{X}(t;\nu)\leq 1+s

(651)

hold over
t\in[0,\infty).
Fix
s\in(0,(1-\mu_{X})/(1+\mu_{X}))
and
1\leq\nu\leq\nu_{X,n,s}.

Now, the integrand in the upper bound in (
647
) can be rewritten as

\frac{\nu f_{X}(\nu t)-g_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(\nu t)}=\varphi_{X}(t;\nu)\left(\frac{f_{X}(t)-g_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}+\frac{\nu f_{X}(\nu t)-f_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}\right).

(652)

The integrand in the lower bound in (
647
) can be rewritten as

\frac{f_{X}(t)-\nu g_{X}(\nu t)}{u_{X}(\nu t)-v_{X}(t)}=\psi_{X}(t;\nu)\left(\frac{f_{X}(t)-g_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}+\frac{g_{X}(t)-\nu g_{X}(\nu t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}\right).

(653)

By the bounds in (
651
), we have that for every
t\geq 0

0\leq\varphi_{X}(t;\nu)-1\leq s.

(654)

Hence, by non-negativity of
f_{X}
and
g_{X},
we deduce

\left(\varphi_{X}(t;\nu)-1\right)\cdot\frac{f_{X}(t)-g_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}\leq s\cdot\frac{f_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)},

(655)

i.e.,

\varphi_{X}(t;\nu)\cdot\frac{f_{X}(t)-g_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}\leq\frac{f_{X}(t)-g_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}+s\cdot\frac{f_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}.

(656)

In addition, since
f_{X}(\nu t)\leq\nu^{d_{n}-2}f_{X}(t)
over
t\in[0,\infty),
inequality (
654
) implies that

\varphi_{X}(t;\nu)\cdot\frac{\nu f_{X}(\nu t)-f_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}\leq\frac{(1+s)(\nu^{d_{n}-1}-1)f_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}.

(657)

Therefore, applying inequalities (
656
) and (
657
) in formula (
652
), we deduce in view of the upper bound in (
647
) the inequality

\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho_{U,n}(t)-\rho_{X,n}(t)\,dt\leq\left((1+s)\nu^{d_{n}-1}-1\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{f_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}\,dt.

(658)

Similarly, we derive a lower bound on (
653
). By (
651
), we have that for every
t\geq 0

s\geq 1-\psi_{X}(t;\nu)\geq 0.

(659)

Hence, by non-negativity of
f_{X}
and
g_{X},

s\cdot\frac{f_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}\geq\left(1-\psi_{X}(t;\nu)\right)\frac{f_{X}(t)-g_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)},

(660)

i.e.,

\psi_{X}(t;\nu)\cdot\frac{f_{X}(t)-g_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}\geq\frac{f_{X}(t)-g_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}-s\cdot\frac{f_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}.

(661)

In addition, from
\psi_{X}(t;\nu)\leq 1\leq\nu
and
g_{X}(\nu t)\leq\nu^{d_{n}-2}g_{X}(t)
for
t\geq 0,
we deduce

\psi_{X}(t;\nu)\cdot\frac{g_{X}(t)-\nu g_{X}(\nu t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}\geq\psi_{X}(t;\nu)\cdot\frac{(1-\nu^{d_{n}-1})g_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}\geq\left(1-\nu^{d_{n}-1}\right)\frac{g_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}.

(662)

Therefore, applying inequalities (
661
) and (
662
) in formula (
653
), the lower bound in (
647
) yields bound

\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho_{U,n}(t)-\rho_{X,n}(t)\,dt\geq-s\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{f_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}\,dt-\left(\nu^{d_{n}-1}-1\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{g_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}\,dt.

(663)

In particular, (
663
) implies that

\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho_{U,n}(t)-\rho_{X,n}(t)\,dt\geq-\left(\nu^{d_{n}-1}-(1-s)\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{f_{X}(t)+g_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}\,dt.

(664)

Now, note that
(1+s)\nu^{d_{n}-1}-1\geq\nu^{d_{n}-1}-(1-s).
Therefore, combining the upper bound in (
658
) and the lower bound in (
664
), we deduce that

\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho_{U,n}(t)-\rho_{X,n}(t)\,dt\right|\leq\left((1+s)\nu^{d_{n}-1}-1\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{f_{X}(t)+g_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}\,dt.

(665)

The upper bound in (
665
) may be made as small as needed by choosing a small
s
then choosing a small
\nu.

The second part of the proof, given in Appendix
K.3
, derives the following error bound for estimating
\log\det\bm{M}_{X,n}
from samples. If
B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})>0,
we denote

\tau_{X,n}:=\left(\frac{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})/B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})+1}{2}\right)^{1/(n+1)}\in(1,\infty)

(666)

and

\eta_{X,n}:=\min\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{\tau_{X,n}-1}{\tau_{X,n}+1}\right)\in(0,1/2].

(667)

If
B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})=0,
then we set
\tau_{X,n}=\infty
and
\eta_{X,n}=1/2.
We show that for all
\eta\in(0,\eta_{X,n}),

if
\mathfrak{A}_{n,\eta}(\mathcal{S})
holds, then we have the bound

\left|\frac{1}{2d_{n}}\log\frac{\det\bm{M}_{U,n}}{\det\bm{M}_{X,n}}\right|\leq\frac{6\eta}{n}\cdot\frac{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})+B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}.

(668)

To finish the proof, we choose
\eta
so that the desired accuracy is achieved with high probability. Recall from (
638
) that

P\left(\mathfrak{A}_{n,\eta}(\mathcal{S})\right)\geq 1-4ne^{-m\eta^{2}\alpha_{X,n}}

(669)

where we denote the constant

\alpha_{X,n}:=2\cdot\left(\left(\frac{q}{p}\right)^{2n}-1\right)^{-2}.

(670)

In addition, from (
665
) and (
668
), we know that if
s\in(0,(1-\mu_{X})/(1+\mu_{X})),

\nu\in[1,\nu_{X,n,s}],

\eta\in(0,\eta_{X,n}),
and
\mathfrak{A}_{n,\eta}(\mathcal{S})
occurs, then

\left|\widehat{h}_{n}(\mathcal{S})-h_{n}(X)\right|\leq\eta\cdot\beta_{X,n}+\left((1+s)\nu^{d_{n}-1}-1\right)\cdot\gamma_{X,n}

(671)

where we denote the constants

\displaystyle\beta_{X,n}

\displaystyle:=\frac{6}{n}\cdot\frac{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})+B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})},

(672)

\displaystyle\gamma_{X,n}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{f_{X}(t)+g_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}\,dt.

(673)

Consider the constant
\varepsilon_{X,n}\in(0,2\gamma_{X,n}]
defined by

\varepsilon_{X,n}:=2\gamma_{X,n}\cdot\frac{1-\mu_{X}}{1+\mu_{X}}.

(674)

Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{X,n}),
set
s:=\varepsilon/(6\gamma_{X,n})\in(0,1/3],
denote

\kappa_{X,n}:=\min\left(3,\tau_{X,n},\left(\frac{1-s\mu_{X}}{1-s}\right)^{1/(2d_{n})},\frac{1+\varepsilon/(2\beta_{X,n})}{1-\varepsilon/(2\beta_{X,n})}\right),

(675)

and fix
\eta\in(0,(\kappa_{X,n}-1)/(\kappa_{X,n}+1)).
Since
\kappa_{X,n}\leq 3,
we obtain
\eta<1/2.
In addition,
\kappa_{X,n}\leq\tau_{X,n},
hence
\eta<(\kappa_{X,n}-1)/(\kappa_{X,n}+1)
implies that
\eta<\eta_{X,n}.
Note that, for
a\in(0,1)
and
b>1,
the inequality
a\leq(b-1)/(b+1)
is equivalent to
(1+a)/(1-a)\leq b.
By definition,

\kappa_{X,n}\leq\left(\frac{1-s\mu_{X}}{1-s}\right)^{1/(2d_{n})},

(676)

hence we have

(1+s)\nu^{d_{n}}=(1+s)\left(\frac{1+\eta}{1-\eta}\right)^{2d}<(1+s)\kappa_{X,n}^{2d}\leq(1+s)\cdot\frac{1-s\mu_{X}}{1-s}\leq\frac{1+s}{1-s}\leq\frac{1+s+s(1-3s)}{1-s}=1+3s.

(677)

In addition, since

\kappa_{X,n}\leq\frac{1+\varepsilon/(2\beta_{X,n})}{1-\varepsilon/(2\beta_{X,n})},

(678)

and since we assume
\eta<(\kappa_{X,n}-1)/(\kappa_{X,n}+1),
we deduce the inequality
\eta<\varepsilon/(2\beta_{X,n}).
Applying the two inequalities
\eta<\varepsilon/(2\beta_{X,n})
and
(1+s)\nu^{d_{n}}\leq 1+3s
(see (
677
)) into inequality (
671
), we conclude that

\left|\widehat{h}_{n}(\mathcal{S})-h_{n}(X)\right|\leq\eta\cdot\beta_{X,n}+\left((1+s)\nu^{d_{n}-1}-1\right)\cdot\gamma_{X,n}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}=\varepsilon

(679)

whenever
\mathfrak{A}_{n,\eta}(\mathcal{S})
occurs.

Now, fix
\delta\in(0,1/(4n)).
Set

\eta:=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{\kappa_{X,n}-1}{\kappa_{X,n}+1}.

(680)

We show that
\eta\geq\varepsilon c_{X,n},
where we denote the constant
c_{X,n}
by

c_{X,n}:=\min\left(\frac{1}{8\gamma_{X,n}},\frac{\tau_{X,n}-1}{4\gamma_{X,n}(\tau_{X,n}+1)},\frac{1-\mu_{X}}{72\gamma_{X,n}d_{n}},\frac{1}{4\beta_{X,n}}\right).

(681)

In this definition of
c_{X,n},
the term involving
\tau_{X,n}
is removed if
\tau_{X,n}=\infty.
We assume that

m\geq\frac{2/(c_{X,n}^{2}\alpha_{X,n})}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log\frac{1}{\delta}.

(682)

From
\eta\geq\varepsilon c_{X,n}
and (
682
), it follows that the probability that the event
\mathfrak{A}_{n,\eta}(\mathcal{S})
does not occur is bounded as

P\left(\mathfrak{A}_{n,\eta}(\mathcal{S})^{c}\right)\leq 4ne^{-m\eta^{2}\alpha_{X,n}}\leq\delta.

(683)

Note that this would conclude the proof, as then we would have that

P\left(\left|\widehat{h}_{n}(\mathcal{S})-h_{n}(X)\right|\leq\varepsilon\right)\geq P\left.\left(\left|\widehat{h}_{n}(\mathcal{S})-h_{n}(X)\right|\leq\varepsilon\penalty\ \right|\penalty\ \mathfrak{A}_{n,\eta}(\mathcal{S})\right)P\left(\mathfrak{A}_{n,\eta}(\mathcal{S})\right)=P\left(\mathfrak{A}_{n,\eta}(\mathcal{S})\right)>1-\delta.

(684)

The rest of the proof is devoted to showing that
\eta\geq\varepsilon c_{X,n}
holds.

Let
\rho=(1-\mu_{X})/(6d_{n}).
We will show that

\left(\frac{1-s\mu_{X}}{1-s}\right)^{1/(2d_{n})}\geq\frac{1+s\rho}{1-s\rho}.

(685)

Inequality (
685
) is equivalent to

(1-s\mu_{X})(1-s\rho)^{2d_{n}}\geq(1+\rho s)^{2d_{n}}(1-s).

(686)

By Bernoulli’s inequality, since
0\leq s\rho\leq 1,
we have that
(1-s\rho)^{2d_{n}}\geq 1-2d_{n}\rho s.
In addition, the inequality
1+2az\geq e^{az}\geq(1+a)^{z}
for
a,z\geq 0
satisfying
az\leq\log 2
implies, in view of
2d_{n}\rho s\leq 1/9<\log 2,
that

1+4d_{n}\rho s\geq(1+\rho)^{2d_{n}}.

(687)

Therefore, to show (
686
), it suffices to show that

(1-s\mu_{X})(1-2d_{n}\rho s)\geq(1+4d_{n}\rho s)(1-s).

(688)

Now, using the definition
\rho=(1-\mu_{X})/(6d_{n}),
inequality (
688
) follows as

\displaystyle(1-s\mu_{X})(1-2d_{n}\rho s)

\displaystyle=(1-s\mu_{X})(1-s(1-\mu_{X})/3)=(1+2(1-\mu_{X})s/3)(1-s)+s^{2}(1-\mu_{X})(\mu_{X}+2)/3

\displaystyle\geq(1+2(1-\mu_{X})s/3)(1-s)=(1+4d_{n}\rho s)(1-s).

(689)

Since (
688
) holds, we conclude that inequality (
685
) holds.

Now, by the definition of
\kappa_{X,n}
in (
675
) there are four possible values
\kappa_{X,n}
can take. First, if
\kappa_{X,n}=3,
then

\eta=\frac{1}{4}=\varepsilon\cdot\frac{1}{4\varepsilon}\geq\varepsilon\cdot\frac{1}{8\gamma_{X,n}}\geq\varepsilon c_{X,n}

(690)

since
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{X,n}\leq 2\gamma_{X,n}.
Now, if
\kappa_{X,n}=\tau_{X,n}
(so
B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})>0
), then

\eta=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{\tau_{X,n}-1}{\tau_{X,n}+1}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{4\gamma_{X,n}}\cdot\frac{\tau_{X,n}-1}{\tau_{X,n}+1}

(691)

since
\varepsilon<2\gamma_{X,n}.
Next, suppose that

\kappa_{X,n}=\left(\frac{1-s\mu_{X}}{1-s}\right)^{1/(2d_{n})}.

(692)

By (
685
) and (
692
), we deduce that

\kappa_{X,n}\geq\frac{1+s\rho}{1-s\rho}.

(693)

Recall that, for
0<a<1<b,
the inequalities
(1+a)/(1-a)\geq b
and
(b-1)/(b+1)\geq a
are equivalent. Therefore, the definition of
\eta
in (
680
) yields from (
693
) that
\eta\geq s\rho/2.
Plugging in the definitions of
s
and
\rho,
we conclude that

\eta\geq\varepsilon\cdot\frac{1-\mu_{X}}{72\gamma_{X,n}d_{n}}\geq\varepsilon c_{X,n}.

(694)

Finally, when

\kappa_{X,n}=\frac{1+\varepsilon/(2\beta_{X,n})}{1-\varepsilon/(2\beta_{X,n})},

(695)

the definition of
\eta
implies that
\eta\geq\varepsilon/(4\beta_{X,n})\geq\varepsilon c_{X,n}.
Combining these four cases, we conclude that we must have
\eta\geq\varepsilon c_{X,n}
independently of the value of
\kappa_{X,n}.
The proof is thus complete.

K.2
Uniform Bounds on
\varphi_{X}
and
\psi_{X}
: Inequalities (
651
)

Being polynomials of degree at most
d_{n}
with non-negative coefficients, the functions
u_{X}
and
v_{X}
satisfy
u_{X}(\nu t)\leq\nu^{d_{n}}u_{X}(t)
and
v_{X}(\nu t)\leq\nu^{d_{n}}v_{X}(t)
for every
\nu\geq 1
and
t\geq 0.
Note also that both
u_{X}
and
v_{X}
are nondecreasing. In addition, we have
v_{X}(t)<u_{X}(t)
for every
t\geq 0,
because
u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)=\det\bm{M}_{\sqrt{t}X+N,n}>0.
We have also shown that
\mu_{X}<1,
where
\mu_{X}
is defined in (
642
) as

\mu_{X}:=\sup_{t\in[0,\infty)}\frac{v_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)}.

(696)

These facts will be enough to deduce the bounds in (
651
).

We show first the bounds on
\varphi_{X}
in (
651
). It suffices to consider the case
\mu_{X}>0,
for otherwise
v_{X}
vanishes identically and
\varphi_{X}\equiv 1
identically. We show that for every
s>0
and
1\leq\nu\leq\nu_{X,n,s}^{\prime},
where
\nu_{X,n,s}^{\prime}:=\left((1/s+1/\mu_{X})/(1/s+1)\right)^{1/d_{n}},
the uniform bound
1\leq\varphi_{X}(t;\nu)\leq 1+s
in (
651
) holds.

Consider the lower bound on
\varphi_{X}.
For every
1\leq\nu<\mu_{X}^{-1/d_{n}},
we have the uniform bound

\frac{v_{X}(\nu t)}{u_{X}(t)}\leq\frac{\nu^{d_{n}}v_{X}(t)}{u_{X}(t)}\leq\nu^{d_{n}}\mu_{X}<1

(697)

over
t\in[0,\infty).
In particular,

u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(\nu t)>0

(698)

for every
1\leq\nu<\mu_{X}^{-1/d_{n}}
and
t\geq 0.
Since
v_{X}
is nondecreasing, we conclude that
\varphi_{X}(t;\nu)=(u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t))/(u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(\nu t))\geq 1
whenever
1\leq\nu<\mu_{X}^{-1/d_{n}}.
Note that
\nu_{X,n,s}^{\prime}<\mu_{X}^{-1/d_{n}}
for every
s>0
since
\mu_{X}\in(0,1).

Next, we show the upper bound on
\varphi_{X}.
Fix
s>0
and
\nu\in[1,\nu_{X,n,s}^{\prime}].
Since
v_{X}(t)/\mu_{X}\leq u_{X}(t),
we have for every
t\geq 0
the bound

v_{X}(\nu t)\leq\nu^{d_{n}}v_{X}(t)\leq\frac{1/s+1/\mu_{X}}{1/s+1}\cdot v_{X}(t)\leq\frac{v_{X}(t)/s+u_{X}(t)}{1/s+1}=v_{X}(t)+\frac{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}{1/s+1}.

(699)

Rearranging (
699
), we obtain the bound

\frac{-1}{1/s+1}\leq\frac{v_{X}(t)-v_{X}(\nu t)}{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}.

(700)

Adding
1
to both sides of (
700
) then inverting, we obtain
\varphi_{X}(t;\nu)\leq 1+s
; for this step, we used the fact that
u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(\nu t)>0,
which follows by (
698
) since
\nu\leq\nu_{X,n,s}^{\prime}<\mu_{X}^{-1/d_{n}}.

Next, we prove the bounds on
\psi_{X}
in (
651
). We do not assume
\mu_{X}>0.
The upper bound
\psi_{X}(t;\nu)\leq 1
follows for every
\nu\geq 1
by monotonicity of
u_{X}.
For the lower bound on
\psi_{X},
we show that for every
s\in(0,1)
and
1\leq\nu\leq\nu_{X,n,s},
where
\nu_{X,n,s}:=((1-s\mu_{X})/(1-s))^{1/d_{n}},
the uniform bound
\psi_{X}(t;\nu)\geq 1-s
holds over
t\in[0,\infty).
We have, for every
s\in(0,1)
and
\nu\in[1,\nu_{X,n,s}],
the bound

u_{X}(\nu t)\leq\nu^{d_{n}}u_{X}(t)\leq\frac{1-s\mu_{X}}{1-s}\cdot u_{X}(t)\leq\frac{u_{X}(t)-sv_{X}(t)}{1-s}=\frac{u_{X}(t)-v_{X}(t)}{1-s}+v_{X}(t)

(701)

over
t\in[0,\infty).
Rearranging (
701
), we obtain
\psi_{X}(t;\nu)\geq 1-s,
as desired.

Finally, note that
\nu_{X,n,s}\leq\nu_{X,n,s}^{\prime}
is equivalent to
s\leq(1-\mu_{X})/(1+\mu_{X}).
This concludes the proof that, for every
s\in(0,(1-\mu_{X})/(1+\mu_{X}))
and
\nu\in[1,\nu_{X,n,s}],
the uniform bounds in (
651
)

1-s\leq\psi_{X}(t;\nu)\leq 1\leq\varphi_{X}(t;\nu)\leq 1+s

(702)

hold over
t\in[0,\infty).

K.3
Error in Estimating
\log\det\bm{M}_{X,n}
: Inequality (
668
)

Recall that

\det\bm{M}_{X,n}=B_{d_{n}}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})=B^{(+)}_{d_{n}}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-B^{(-)}_{d_{n}}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}).

(703)

We bound the error when estimating
\log\det\bm{M}_{X,n}
from the samples
\mathcal{S}.
Denote the random vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}:=\left(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}}{m},\cdots,\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}^{2n}}{m}\right),
and note that

\det\bm{M}_{U,n}=B_{d_{n}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})=B^{(+)}_{d_{n}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})-B^{(-)}_{d_{n}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}).

(704)

We assume that
m>n.
Let
\eta_{X,n}
be as defined by (
666
) and (
667
), and fix
\eta\in(0,\eta_{X,n}).
Then we show that under
\mathfrak{A}_{n,\eta}(\mathcal{S})

\left|\frac{1}{2d_{n}}\log\frac{\det\bm{M}_{U,n}}{\det\bm{M}_{X,n}}\right|\leq\frac{6\eta}{n}\cdot\frac{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})+B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}.

(705)

By (
179
) in Theorem
18
, each term in the polynomials
B_{d_{n}}^{(\pm)}
is a product of at most
n+1
monomials. Thus,

(1-\eta)^{n+1}B_{d_{n}}^{(\pm)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})\leq B_{d_{n}}^{(\pm)}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\leq(1+\eta)^{n+1}B_{d_{n}}^{(\pm)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}).

(706)

It suffices to consider the case when
B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}
is not the zero polynomial, for if
B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}
is the zero polynomial then we obtain from (
668
) the bound

\left|\frac{1}{2d_{n}}\log\frac{\det\bm{M}_{U,n}}{\det\bm{M}_{X,n}}\right|=\frac{1}{2d_{n}}\left|\log\frac{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mu})}{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}\right|\leq\frac{\max\left(\log(1+\eta),-\log(1-\eta)\right)}{n}=\frac{-\log(1-\eta)}{n}<\frac{2\eta}{n}

(707)

where the last inequality follow because
-\log(1-z)<2z
for
z\in(0,1/2),
which can be verified by checking the derivative. Note that the bound
2\eta/n
in (
707
) is stronger than the bound in (
705
). Assume that
B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}
does not vanish identically, so positivity of
X
yields that
B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})>0.

From (
706
), we have that

\displaystyle\log\frac{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-\nu^{\frac{n+1}{2}}B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}+(n+1)\log(1-\eta)\leq\log\frac{\det\bm{M}_{U,n}}{\det\bm{M}_{X,n}}

(708)

and

\displaystyle\log\frac{\det\bm{M}_{U,n}}{\det\bm{M}_{X,n}}\leq\log\frac{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-\nu^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}+(n+1)\log(1+\eta)

(709)

where we used our assumption that

\nu^{\frac{n+1}{2}}=\left(1+\frac{2}{1/\eta-1}\right)^{n+1}<\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}{B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}+1\right)<\frac{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}{B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}.

(710)

Now, for every
(w,z,r)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that
w>z>0
and
w/z>r>1,
rearranging
r+1/r>2
we have that

\frac{w-z/r}{w-z}<\frac{w-z}{w-rz}.

(711)

Setting
(w,z,r)=(B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}),B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}),\nu^{(n+1)/2}),
we obtain that

1<\frac{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-\nu^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}<\frac{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-\nu^{\frac{n+1}{2}}B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}.

(712)

Therefore,

0<\log\frac{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-\nu^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}<\left|\log\frac{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-\nu^{\frac{n+1}{2}}B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}\right|.

(713)

Applying (
713
) in (
709
) and combining that with (
708
), we obtain (since
\log(1+\eta)<-\log(1-\eta)
) the bound

\left|\log\frac{\det\bm{M}_{U,n}}{\det\bm{M}_{X,n}}\right|\leq\log\frac{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-\nu^{\frac{n+1}{2}}B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}+(n+1)\log\frac{1}{1-\eta}.

(714)

Now, we may write

\frac{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-\nu^{\frac{n+1}{2}}B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}=\left(1-\frac{B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}\left(\nu^{\frac{n+1}{2}}-1\right)\right)^{-1}.

(715)

The proof of (
705
) is completed by showing that for
(w,z,r)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{3}
such that
(1+z)^{r}<1+\frac{1}{2w}
we have

-\log\left(1-w\left((1+z)^{r}-1\right)\right)\leq(2w+1)rz.

(716)

Before showing that (
716
) holds, we note how it completes the proof. Setting

(w,z,r)=\left(\frac{B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})},\frac{2\eta}{1-\eta},n+1\right),

(717)

we obtain that

\log\frac{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-\nu^{\frac{n+1}{2}}B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}\leq\frac{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})+B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}\cdot(n+1)\cdot\frac{2\eta}{1-\eta}

(718)

since

\nu^{\frac{n+1}{2}}<\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}{B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}+1\right).

(719)

Then
-\log(1-\eta)<2\eta
yields from (
714
) and (
718
) that

\frac{1}{2d_{n}}\left|\log\frac{\det\bm{M}_{U,n}}{\det\bm{M}_{X,n}}\right|\leq\frac{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})+B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}{B_{d_{n}}^{(+)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})-B_{d_{n}}^{(-)}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}})}\cdot\frac{2\eta}{n(1-\eta)}+\frac{2\eta}{n}.

(720)

Then (
718
) yields the desired inequality (
668
) as
\eta\in(0,1/2).

Finally, to see that (
716
) holds, we consider for fixed
w,r>0

f(z):=(2w+1)rz+\log\left(1-w\left(\left(1+z\right)^{r}-1\right)\right)

(721)

over
0\leq z<(1+1/(2w))^{1/r}-1.
Inequality (
716
) is restated as
f(z)\geq 0
for every
0<z<(1+1/(2w))^{1/r}-1,
which follows since
f
is continuous,
f(0)=0,

f^{\prime}(0^{+})=(w+1)r>0,
and

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(z)

\displaystyle=(2w+1)r-\frac{wr(1+z)^{r-1}}{1-w((1+z)^{r}-1)}>(2w+1)r-\frac{wr(1+z)^{r}}{1-w((1+z)^{r}-1)}

(722)

\displaystyle>(2w+1)r-\frac{wr(1+1/(2w))}{1-w((1+1/(2w))-1)}=0

(723)

for every
0\leq z<(1+1/(2w))^{1/r}-1.

K.4
Proof of Proposition
51
: Mutual Information

Let
\{(X_{j},Y_{j})\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
be i.i.d. samples drawn according to
P_{X,Y}.
Denote
\mathcal{S}_{m}=\{X_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}.
By continuity of
Y,
we may assume that all the
Y_{j},
for
j\in\mathbb{N},
are distinct. For each
x\in\mathrm{supp}(X),
let
J_{x}:=\{1\leq j\leq m\penalty\ ;\penalty\ X_{j}=x\}.
Let
\mathfrak{D}_{m}
be the event that, for every
x\in\mathrm{supp}(X),
we have that
|J_{x}|>n.
We use Hoeffding’s inequality to obtain a lower bound on the probability

P(\mathfrak{D}_{m})=P\left(\min_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)}|J_{x}|>n\right).

(724)

Let
\widehat{P}_{m}
be the empirical measure:
\widehat{P}_{m}(x):=m^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\delta_{x}(X_{j}).
Note that
|J_{x}|=m\widehat{P}_{m}(x).

Let
x_{0}\in\mathrm{supp}(X)
be such that
P_{X}(x_{0})
is minimal, set
\zeta:=P_{X}(x_{0})/2,
and suppose
m\geq\zeta^{-1}n.
Then, the union bound and
\zeta\leq P_{X}(x)-\zeta
for each
x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)
yield that

\displaystyle P\left(n\geq\min_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)}|J_{x}|\right)

\displaystyle\leq P\left(m\zeta\geq\min_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)}|J_{x}|\right)\leq\sum_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)}P\left(m\zeta\geq|J_{x}|\right)

(725)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)}P\left(m(P_{X}(x)-\zeta)\geq|J_{x}|\right)=\sum_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)}P\left(P_{X}(x)-\widehat{P}_{m}(x)\geq\zeta\right).

(726)

Since
\mathbb{E}[\widehat{P}_{m}(x)]=P_{X}(x)
for each
x\in\mathrm{supp}(X),
Hoeffding’s inequality yields that
P\left(P_{X}(x)-\widehat{P}_{m}(x)\geq\zeta\right)\leq e^{-2\zeta^{2}m}.
Therefore,

P\left(n\geq\min_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)}|J_{x}|\right)\leq|\mathrm{supp}(X)|\cdot e^{-2\zeta^{2}m}.

(727)

In other words, for every
m\geq 2n/P_{X}(x_{0}),
we have the bound

P(\mathfrak{D}_{m})\geq 1-|\mathrm{supp}(X)|\cdot e^{-mP_{X}(x_{0})^{2}/2}.

(728)

Denote
\pi_{X}:=4/P_{X}(x_{0})^{2}
and

\delta_{X,n}:=\min\left(\frac{1}{4|\mathrm{supp}(X)|},e^{-P_{X}(x_{0})n/2}\right).

(729)

We conclude from (
728
) that, for every
\delta\in(0,\delta_{X,n}),
if
m\geq\pi_{X}\log(1/\delta)
then
P(\mathfrak{D}_{m})>1-\delta/4.

Consider the event
\mathfrak{P}_{m,\varepsilon}
that the empirical measure
\widehat{P}_{m}
is pointwise
\varepsilon
-close to the true measure
P_{X},
i.e.,

\mathfrak{P}_{m,\varepsilon}:=\left\{\max_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)}\left|\widehat{P}_{m}(x)-P_{X}(x)\right|<\varepsilon\right\}.

(730)

By the union bound, we have that

P\left(\mathfrak{P}_{m,\varepsilon}^{c}\right)\leq\sum_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)}P\left(\left|\widehat{P}_{m}(x)-P_{X}(x)\right|\geq\varepsilon\right).

(731)

By Hoeffding’s inequality, for each
x\in\mathrm{supp}(X),
we have that

P\left(\left|\widehat{P}_{m}(x)-P_{X}(x)\right|\geq\varepsilon\right)\leq 2e^{-2m\varepsilon^{2}}.

(732)

Therefore, we obtain the bound

P\left(\mathfrak{P}_{m,\varepsilon}\right)>1-2|\mathrm{supp}(X)|e^{-2m\varepsilon^{2}}.

(733)

In particular, if
\delta\in(0,1/(4|\mathrm{supp}(X)|)),
then
m\geq(1/\varepsilon^{2})\log(1/\delta)
implies
P(\mathfrak{B}_{m,\varepsilon})>1-\delta/2.

Recall that, if
\mathfrak{D}_{m}
occurs, then we may write

\widehat{I}_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{m})=\widehat{h}_{n}(\mathcal{A}_{m})-\sum_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)}\widehat{P}_{m}(x)\penalty\ \widehat{h}_{n}(\mathcal{B}_{m,x}),

(734)

where
\mathcal{A}_{m}:=\{Y_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
and
\mathcal{B}_{m,x}:=\{Y_{j}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ 1\leq j\leq m,X_{j}=x\}.
Then,

\displaystyle\left|\widehat{I}_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{m})-I_{n}(X;Y)\right|\leq\left|\widehat{h}_{n}(\mathcal{A}_{m})-h_{n}(Y)\right|

\displaystyle+\sum_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)}\widehat{P}_{m}(x)\left|\widehat{h}_{n}(\mathcal{B}_{m,x})-h_{n}(Y^{(x)})\right|

\displaystyle+\left(\max_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)}\left|\widehat{P}_{m}(x)-P_{X}(x)\right|\right)\sum_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)}|h_{n}(Y^{(x)})|.

(735)

Denote
H_{X,Y,n}:=\sum_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)}|h_{n}(Y^{(x)})|.
Consider the events

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}_{x,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=\bigcap_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)}\left\{\left|\widehat{h}_{n}(\mathcal{B}_{m,x})-h_{n}(Y^{(x)})\right|<\frac{\varepsilon}{3}\right\}

(736)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\left|\widehat{h}_{n}(\mathcal{A}_{m})-h_{n}(Y)\right|<\frac{\varepsilon}{3}\right\}.

(737)

Set
\mathfrak{H}_{\varepsilon}:=\bigcap_{x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)}\mathfrak{H}_{x,\varepsilon}.
From Theorem
48
, we know that there is a constant
C_{X,Y,n}
such that for every small enough
\varepsilon,\delta>0,
if
m\geq(C_{X,Y,n}/\varepsilon^{2})\log(1/\delta)
then
P(\mathfrak{H}_{x,\varepsilon}\mid\mathfrak{D}_{m})\geq 1-\delta/(8|\mathrm{supp}(X)|)
for each
x\in\mathrm{supp}(X)
and
P(\mathfrak{H}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mid\mathfrak{D}_{m})>1-\delta/8.
Then,
P(\mathfrak{H}_{\varepsilon}\cap\mathfrak{H}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mid\mathfrak{D}_{m})\geq 1-\delta/4.
We conclude, possibly after increasing
C_{X,Y,n},
that
P(\mathfrak{H}_{\varepsilon}\cap\mathfrak{H}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\cap\mathfrak{D}_{m})\geq 1-\delta/2.
Also,
P(\mathfrak{B}_{m,\varepsilon/(2H_{X,Y,n})})>1-\delta/2.
Then,
P(\mathfrak{H}_{\varepsilon}\cap\mathfrak{H}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\cap\mathfrak{D}_{m}\cap\mathfrak{B}_{m,\varepsilon/(2H_{X,Y,n})})\geq 1-\delta.
But under the event
\mathfrak{H}_{\varepsilon}\cap\mathfrak{H}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\cap\mathfrak{D}_{m}\cap\mathfrak{B}_{m,\varepsilon/(2H_{X,Y,n})},
we have the bound

\left|\widehat{I}_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{m})-I_{n}(X;Y)\right|<\varepsilon,

(738)

and the proof is complete.

Appendix L
Algebraic Proof of Corollary
25

We provide here an alternative algebraic proof of the formulas
a_{X}^{n,d_{n}-1}=\det\bm{M}_{X,n}
and
a_{X}^{n,d_{n}}=0
(see equations (
212
)–(
214
) for the definition of these quantities). These equations were proved in Section
5
by invoking that
\mathrm{mmse}(X,t)\sim 1/t
(as
t\to\infty
) for a continuous RV
X.
Ultimately, via Proposition
24
, we have shown that these formulas hold identically as polynomials in the symbols
\{\mathcal{X}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{2n}.
The alternative proof we present in this appendix directly derives this latter result by algebraic means without appealing to the MMSE asymptotic result. The point of including this alternative proof is that it might shed light on deriving simple expressions for the other constants
a_{X}^{n,j}
for
1\leq j\leq d_{n}-2
and
b_{X}^{n,\ell}
for
2\leq\ell\leq d_{n}-1.
See Remark
14
for the polynomial expressions of the
a_{X}^{n,j}.

We consider indeterminates
\mathcal{R}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{R}_{2n},
which we think of as moments of a RV
R.
For a permutation
\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}
and integers
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\{i_{1},\cdots,i_{m}\}\subseteq[n],
it will be convenient to denote the products

Q_{R}(\pi;i_{1},\cdots,i_{m}):=\prod_{k\not\in\{i_{1},\cdots,i_{m}\}}\mathcal{R}_{k+\pi(k)},

(739)

and
Q_{R}(\pi):=\prod_{k\in[n]}\mathcal{R}_{k+\pi(k)}.
We let
T_{n}^{(i,j)}\subset\mathcal{S}_{[n]}
denote the subset of permutations that send
i
to
j,
i.e.,

T_{n}^{(i,j)}:=\{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ \pi(i)=j\}.

(740)

Note that, for each fixed
i\in[n],
we have a partition

\mathcal{S}_{[n]}=\bigcup_{j\in[n]}T_{n}^{(i,j)}.

(741)

We will denote for
i\in[n]
and
\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}
the composition of permutations

\pi_{i}:=\pi\circ(1\;i).

(742)

If
i=1,
then
\pi_{i}=\pi.
Further, for each
i\in[n],
as multiplication by
(1\;i)
is an automorphism of
\mathcal{S}_{[n]},
the mapping
\pi\mapsto\pi_{i}
is a bijection of
\mathcal{S}_{[n]}.
In addition, when
i\neq 1,

\mathrm{sgn}(\pi_{i})=-\mathrm{sgn}(\pi).

(743)

L.1
Leading Coefficients

We first show that the coefficient of
t^{d_{n}}
in
F_{R,n}(t)
is
\mathcal{R}_{2}\det\bm{M}_{R,n},
i.e., that

\sum_{i\in[n]}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\mathcal{R}_{i+1}\mathcal{R}_{\pi(i)+1}Q_{R}(\pi;i)=\mathcal{R}_{2}\det\bm{M}_{R,n}.

(744)

For each
i\in[n]\setminus\{1\},
we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\mathcal{R}_{\pi(i)+1}Q_{R}(\pi;i)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\mathcal{R}_{\pi(i)+1}\mathcal{R}_{\pi(1)+1}Q_{R}(\pi;1,i)

(745)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi_{i})\mathcal{R}_{\pi_{i}(i)+1}\mathcal{R}_{\pi_{i}(1)+1}Q_{R}(\pi_{i};1,i)

(746)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\mathcal{R}_{\pi(1)+1}\mathcal{R}_{\pi(i)+1}Q_{R}(\pi;1,i)

(747)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\mathcal{R}_{\pi(i)+1}Q_{R}(\pi;i).

(748)

Hence,

\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\mathcal{R}_{\pi(i)+1}Q_{R}(\pi;i)=0.

(749)

Thus, only
i=1
could give a nonzero sum in the left hand side of (
744
). Furthermore, when
i=1
in (
744
), we obtain the sum

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\mathcal{R}_{2}\mathcal{R}_{\pi(1)+1}Q_{R}(\pi;1)=\mathcal{R}_{2}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)Q_{R}(\pi)=\mathcal{R}_{2}\det\bm{M}_{R,n}.

(750)

Thus, (
744
) follows. In view of equation (
209
) in Lemma
21
, equation (
744
) yields that
a_{R}^{n,d_{n}}=0.
Next, we apply similar bijectivity tricks to show that
a_{R}^{n,d_{n}-1}=\det\bm{M}_{R,n}.

Via the application of Leibniz’s formula in equation (
203
), a preliminary formula for
a_{R}^{n,d_{n}-1}
is as follows

\displaystyle a_{R}^{n,d_{n}-1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{2}\sum_{r\in[n]}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\binom{r+\pi(r)}{2}\mathcal{R}_{r+\pi(r)-2}Q_{R}(\pi;r)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-\sum_{(i,j)\in[n]^{2}}\sum_{\pi\in T_{n}^{(i,j)}}\sum_{r\neq i}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\binom{r+\pi(r)}{2}\mathcal{R}_{i+1}\mathcal{R}_{j+1}\mathcal{R}_{r+\pi(r)-2}Q_{R}(\pi;i,r)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-\sum_{(i,j)\in[n]^{2}}\sum_{\pi\in T_{n}^{(i,j)}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)Q_{R}(\pi;i)\left(\binom{i}{2}\mathcal{R}_{i-1}\mathcal{R}_{j+1}+\binom{j}{2}\mathcal{R}_{i+1}\mathcal{R}_{j-1}\right),

(751)

where we set
\mathcal{R}_{\ell}=0
when
\ell<0.
We will deal with each of the three sums in this preliminary formula separately; so, denote the three sums, in order, by
\mathfrak{S}_{1},\mathfrak{S}_{2},\mathfrak{S}_{3}
(where, for
\mathfrak{S}_{1},
we absorb the factor
\mathcal{R}_{2}
inside the sum), i.e., define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{r\in[n]}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\binom{r+\pi(r)}{2}\mathcal{R}_{2}\mathcal{R}_{r+\pi(r)-2}Q_{R}(\pi;r),

(752)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{2}

\displaystyle:=-\sum_{(i,j)\in[n]^{2}}\sum_{\pi\in T_{n}^{(i,j)}}\sum_{r\neq i}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\binom{r+\pi(r)}{2}\mathcal{R}_{i+1}\mathcal{R}_{j+1}\mathcal{R}_{r+\pi(r)-2}Q_{R}(\pi;i,r),

(753)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{3}

\displaystyle:=-\sum_{(i,j)\in[n]^{2}}\sum_{\pi\in T_{n}^{(i,j)}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)Q_{R}(\pi;i)\left(\binom{i}{2}\mathcal{R}_{i-1}\mathcal{R}_{j+1}+\binom{j}{2}\mathcal{R}_{i+1}\mathcal{R}_{j-1}\right).

(754)

Thus,
a_{R}^{n,d_{n}-1}=\mathfrak{S}_{1}+\mathfrak{S}_{2}+\mathfrak{S}_{3}.
We show that this coefficient is equal to
\det\bm{M}_{R,n}
by showing that

\mathfrak{S}_{1}+\mathfrak{S}_{2}=\det\bm{M}_{R,n}

(755)

and that

\mathfrak{S}_{3}=0.

(756)

For
i_{1},\cdots,i_{\ell}\in[n],
let
[n]_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{\ell}}:=[n]\setminus\{i_{1},\cdots,i_{\ell}\}.

For the first sum,
\mathfrak{S}_{1},
we partition
[n]\times\mathcal{S}_{[n]}
into three parts as

[n]\times\mathcal{S}_{[n]}=\left(\{1\}\times T_{n}^{(1,1)}\right)\cup\left(\{1\}\times\bigcup_{j\in[n]_{1}}T_{n}^{(1,j)}\right)\cup\left([n]_{1}\times\mathcal{S}_{[n]}\right),

(757)

and we let
\mathfrak{S}_{1}=\mathfrak{S}_{1,1}+\mathfrak{S}_{1,2}+\mathfrak{S}_{1,3}
be the ensuing decomposition, which we express next. For the first part in (
757
), we obtain

\mathfrak{S}_{1,1}=\sum_{\pi\in T_{n}^{(1,1)}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)Q_{R}(\pi),

(758)

whereas the second and third parts give

\mathfrak{S}_{1,2}=\sum_{j\in[n]_{1}}\sum_{\pi\in T_{n}^{(1,j)}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\binom{j+1}{2}\mathcal{R}_{2}\mathcal{R}_{j-1}Q_{R}(\pi;1)

(759)

and

\mathfrak{S}_{1,3}=\hskip-8.53581pt\sum_{(r,\pi)\in[n]_{1}\times\mathcal{S}_{[n]}}\hskip-8.53581pt\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\binom{r+\pi(r)}{2}\mathcal{R}_{2}\mathcal{R}_{r+\pi(r)-2}Q_{R}(\pi;r),

(760)

respectively. We will show that both
\mathfrak{S}_{1,2}
and
\mathfrak{S}_{1,3}
cancel out identically when summed with parts of the sum
\mathfrak{S}_{2}.
We also note that
\mathfrak{S}_{1,1}
provides part of the sum that will ultimately produce
\det\bm{M}_{R,n}
; the remaining part lies in
\mathfrak{S}_{2},
which we treat next.

Let

U_{n}^{(i,j)}=\left\{(i,j,\pi)\;;\;\pi\in T_{n}^{(i,j)}\right\}.

(761)

For the second sum,
\mathfrak{S}_{2},
we employ the partition

\bigcup_{(i,j)\in[n]^{2}}\left(U_{n}^{(i,j)}\times[n]_{i}\right)=\mathfrak{p}_{1}\cup\mathfrak{p}_{2}\cup\mathfrak{p}_{3}\cup\mathfrak{p}_{4}

(762)

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{j\in[n]}\left(U_{n}^{(1,j)}\times[n]_{1}\right)

(763)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{i\in[n]_{1}}\left(U_{n}^{(i,1)}\times\{1\}\right)

(764)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{3}

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{(i,j)\in[n]_{1}\times[n]}\left(U_{n}^{(i,j)}\times[n]_{1,i}\right)

(765)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{4}

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{(i,j)\in[n]_{1}^{2}}\left(U_{n}^{(i,j)}\times\{1\}\right).

(766)

We will denote the ensuing sums by
\mathfrak{S}_{2,1},\mathfrak{S}_{2,2},\mathfrak{S}_{2,3},\mathfrak{S}_{2,4},
which we express next. We will denote a generic element
((i,j,\pi),r)\in U_{n}^{(i,j)}\times[n]_{i}
as
(i,j,\pi,r)
for short. The
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
-part yields

\mathfrak{S}_{2,1}=-\sum_{(r,\pi)\in[n]_{1}\times\mathcal{S}_{[n]}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\binom{r+\pi(r)}{2}\mathcal{R}_{2}\mathcal{R}_{r+\pi(r)-2}Q_{R}(\pi;r),

(767)

the
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
-part yields

\mathfrak{S}_{2,2}=-\sum_{i\in[n]_{1}}\sum_{\pi\in T_{n}^{(i,1)}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\binom{\pi(1)+1}{2}\mathcal{R}_{2}\mathcal{R}_{\pi(1)-1}Q_{R}(\pi;1),

(768)

and the
\mathfrak{p}_{3}
-part yields

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{2,3}=-

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]_{1}}\sum_{r\in[n]_{1,i}}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{[n]}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\binom{r+\pi(r)}{2}\mathcal{R}_{i+1}\mathcal{R}_{\pi(i)+1}\mathcal{R}_{r+\pi(r)-2}Q_{R}(\pi;i,r).

(769)

We further partition the
\mathfrak{p}_{4}
-part according to whether permutations fix
1,
namely,

\mathfrak{p}_{4}=\mathfrak{p}_{4,1}\cup\mathfrak{p}_{4,2}

(770)

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{4,1}

\displaystyle:=\left\{(i,j,\pi,1)\in\mathfrak{p}_{4}\;;\;\pi(1)=1\right\}

(771)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{4,2}

\displaystyle:=\left\{(i,j,\pi,1)\in\mathfrak{p}_{4}\;;\;\pi(1)\neq 1\right\}.

(772)

We denote the ensuing sums by
\mathfrak{S}_{2,4,1},\mathfrak{S}_{2,4,2}.
The
\mathfrak{p}_{4,1}
-part gives

\mathfrak{S}_{2,4,1}=-\sum_{(i,j)\in[n]_{1}^{2}}\sum_{\pi\in T_{n}^{(i,j)}\cap T_{n}^{(1,1)}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\mathcal{R}_{i+1}\mathcal{R}_{\pi(i)+1}Q_{R}(\pi;i,1),

(773)

whereas the
\mathfrak{p}_{4,2}
-part gives

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{2,4,2}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{(i,j,k)\in[n]_{1}^{3}}\sum_{\pi\in T_{n}^{(i,j)}\cap T_{n}^{(1,k)}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\binom{\pi(1)+1}{2}\mathcal{R}_{i+1}\mathcal{R}_{\pi(i)+1}\mathcal{R}_{\pi(1)-1}Q_{R}(\pi;1,i).

(774)

With this decomposition of
a_{R}^{n,d_{n}-1}
at hand, we proceed to show that equations (
755
) and (
756
) hold by showing that the following six equations hold. We will show that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{1,1}+\mathfrak{S}_{2,4,1}

\displaystyle=\det\bm{M}_{R,n},

(775)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{1,2}+\mathfrak{S}_{2,2}

\displaystyle=0,

(776)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{1,3}+\mathfrak{S}_{2,1}

\displaystyle=0,

(777)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{2,3}

\displaystyle=0,

(778)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{2,4,2}

\displaystyle=0,

(779)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{3}

\displaystyle=0.

(780)

Summing (
775
)–(
780
) gives
a_{R}^{n,d_{n}-1}=\det\bm{M}_{R,n}.

We first show that (
775
) holds. From (
758
), we have that

\mathfrak{S}_{1,1}=\sum_{\pi\in T_{n}^{(1,1)}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)Q_{R}(\pi).

(781)

We show that
\mathfrak{S}_{2,4,1}
complements this summation to give
\det\bm{M}_{R,n},
i.e., that
(\ref{cs})
